> DEVELOPER

i want to incorporate these writing guidelines into the appropraite artefacts that produce projects/tasks/milestones. they should be incorporated in two phases: 1) as the agent is going to write content and b) in a sub-agent review workflow when an agent has finished writing so that its work gets refined

/idea-refine 

Writing Guidelines for AI Agents
Use these rules for software engineering and product management writing.
Core Rules
Put the actor in the subject and the action in the verb. Prefer "The API caches responses" over "Caching is implemented by the API."
Use concrete verbs. Replace nominalizations such as "implementation," "utilization," and "optimization" with "implement," "use," and "optimize."
Make each goal, milestone, or task produce a visible work product: a shipped change, design doc, verified fix, report, migration, test, or decision.
Start with known context and end with the new or important information.
Cut filler, but keep assumptions, risks, constraints, dependencies, owners, dates, and success criteria.
Write like a professional colleague. Prefer a clear story over a list of disconnected facts.
Project Goals and OKRs
Explain the gap between the current state and the desired state.
Use Situation, Complication, Question, Answer logic internally, but do not use those labels as headings.
Make success measurable.
Bad:
"Improve the efficiency of our API and utilize better caching."
Good:
"Our API serves 1k RPS today. The Q3 launch requires 10k RPS, which will exceed current capacity. We will re-architect the load balancer to support 15k RPS."
Bad:
"Significantly improve dashboard speed."
Good:
"Reduce P95 dashboard load time from 4.5s to under 800ms using Edge Workers."
Milestones and Deliverables
Use milestones as summary achievements, not labels.
Group work into non-overlapping containers.
Order groups by sequence, system structure, or impact.
Bad:
"Frontend; Data; Implementation."
Good:
"Built responsive React components; Migrated user records to a sharded Postgres cluster."
Bad:
"Security; Authentication; Login Page."
Good:
"Identity management; Data encryption at rest."
Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors
Every task needs a concrete "done when" state.
Avoid blank tasks such as "Investigate latency" unless the output is named.
Use active voice.
Bad:
"Perform an investigation into the latency situation."
Good:
"Done when: The report identifies the three highest-latency queries in the /orders endpoint."
Bad:
"As a user, I want the utilization of an automated system."
Good:
"As a user, I want to reset my password through an email link so I can regain access in under 2 minutes."
Vocabulary
Use exact technical terms when they are accurate. Replace metaphors only when they hide the cause.
Prefer "essential" or "required" over vague intensifiers such as "vital."
Replace "bottleneck" with the specific shortage, delay, queue, lock, or capacity limit when known.
Prefer "plan" or "spec" over "blueprint" unless discussing an actual final design.
Prefer "limit" or "maximum" over "ceiling."
Prefer "use" over "utilization."
Prefer "implement" over "implementation" when describing action.
Prefer "communicate with," "connect to," or "call" over "interface with."
Prefer "affect" over "impact" as a verb.
Delete "basically," "actually," "quite," "it should be noted that," and similar throat-clearing.
Replace "at this point in time" with "now."
Agent Review Checklist
Before returning an artifact, check:
Does it move from problem to solution in a clear order?
Do subtasks explain how to achieve the milestone or why the milestone matters?
Did pruning preserve assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria?
Is every task's done state visible and verifiable?
Do important terms or conclusions appear where readers will notice them?
Does it sound like a capable colleague wrote it?

=== initiative-shape ===
---
name: initiative-shape
description: "Shapes a vague idea into a properly formed initiative with a clear goal and measurable criteria for success"
---

# Initiative shape

## Purpose

Shape a vague idea into an initiative before work begins, so the work is governed by observable outcomes instead of becoming a repo-aliased backlog. The skill owns the product shape: Goal, Key results, Affected repos, Appetite, Kill condition, and Project type. A tracker binding may record the result, but it does not define the shape.

## When to use

- Starting work expected to span 5 or more issues.
- Converting an existing repo-project into a goal-oriented initiative.
- Preparing initiatives for an upcoming cycle, once per initiative.
- "I want to work on X" where X is bigger than a single issue or bug fix.

## Do not use when

- The work is a single issue, bug, or KTLO item.
- The initiative is already formed and needs deliverables, nodes, or tasks. Use `delivery-shape`.

## Inputs

The user may provide a sentence, project name, direction, problem fragment, or list of existing open issues. Do not require a preformatted brief. Probe for the missing pieces.

## Outputs

A confirmed **initiative shape** that passes the Step 8 verification rubric.

=== delivery-shape ===
---
name: delivery-shape
description: "Decomposes committed work - an initiative, single task, or accepted design doc - into an ordered, verifiable delivery hierarchy: deliverables -> nodes -> tasks."
---

# Delivery shape

## Purpose

Take committed work and create a delivery plan that's easy for human's to review and for agents to action.

The input may be an initiative, a single task, or an accepted design doc. The hierarchy has three layers: deliverables, nodes, and tasks. Deliverables serve a stated outcome source. Nodes are typed units of work such as stories, spikes, ADRs, experiments, design docs, or KTLO work. Tasks are the verifiable checklist inside a node.

## When to use

- Committed work exists and the desired outcome is known.
- You need a deliverable → node → task hierarchy before build pickup.
- You want traceability from each deliverable back to the relevant outcome source.
- You are shaping work from one of these sources:
  - an initiative with a goal and KRs
  - a single task with acceptance criteria or a clear desired outcome
  - an accepted design doc with decisions, constraints, and rollout target

## Do not use when

- The input is only a vague idea or problem with no committed outcome. Route to the appropriate shaping skill first.
- The work is already fully decomposed and ready to execute.
- The request is just to implement one issue now, with no need for an intermediate delivery plan.

## Inputs

=== design-doc ===
---
name: design-doc
description: Produce a structured design document before any non-trivial implementation begins. Use when someone asks "how should we build X", "what's the architecture for Y", "should we build or buy Z", or when work breaks into more than ~5 verifiable slices, contains a one-way-door decision, touches shared infrastructure, or meaningfully affects users, cost, or compliance — even if no doc was explicitly requested. Trigger phrases: "how do we build", "design this", "architecture for", "technical approach to", "what's the plan for building", "let's spec this out".
---

# Design doc

## Purpose

Produce a structured design document — Problem, Context, Constraints, Alternatives, Recommended approach, Consequences, Operability, Open questions — before any non-trivial implementation begins.

The structure forces problem-first reasoning: the doc cannot be finished without working through the problem, the alternatives, and the operability plan in order. The deliverable is the document reviewers evaluate before any code is written, not the code itself.

## When to use

At least one trigger must fire; any single one is sufficient:

1. The work breaks into more than roughly five independently verifiable slices, or contains a one-way-door decision (schema migration, public API, vendor lock-in, auth boundary, production-data touch).
2. The capability will be used by parties outside the team building it.
3. The change has meaningful impact on users, operational cost, or compliance posture.

If a trigger is ambiguous, default to writing the doc. A short, fast doc costs less than a build that starts on a misunderstood problem.

## Do not use when

- No trigger fires — write a short ADR instead. A design doc for sub-threshold work is overhead.
- The problem is not yet understood — run a spike first, then return. A design doc for an undefined problem describes the solution you assumed, not the problem you have.
- The change is purely additive, clearly reversible, and has no shared-interface surface — proceed directly to implementation.

## Inputs

# Sub-agent persona anatomy

The authoring spec for sub-agent personas in `agents/<name>/`.

Sub-agent personas are review passes invoked with fresh context. They are the right tool when judgement is required and the original agent has structural reasons not to catch its own failure (it claimed success, it picked the exciting tech, it solved the easier problem). They are the wrong tool for mechanical checks (use a hook) or for general guidance (embed the principle in the relevant skill).

## Frontmatter

```yaml
---
name: <agent-name>
description: <1-2 sentences>
category: sub-agent
pack: <product | engineering | meta>
review_target: <design-doc | implementation | spec | other>
model: <Fast|Balanced|Frontier>
principles_implemented:
  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
    id: <principle or rule id>
    bucket: sub-agent
length_target: <e.g. 150-200>
author: Anton Babushkin
predecessor:
  repo: <URL or "none">
  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
---
```

## Sections

In this exact order:

1. **Title** — `# <Persona Name>`
2. **Purpose** — one paragraph: what this review catches and why the original agent can't
3. **Review posture** — the stance this persona takes (adversarial, fresh-eyes, no investment in the original work). Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss.
4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output. The point is to form an independent view first, then check it against the work.
5. **Trigger** — when this review fires
6. **Inputs** — what the persona is given
7. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and structured notes
8. **Workflow** — numbered steps the persona walks through
9. **Common rationalisations** — patterns the *original* agent will use that this persona is on the lookout for
10. **Red flags** — signals that warrant a `reject`
11. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane
12. **References** — principles enforced

## Model tier

Every persona declares `model:` in frontmatter. The tier follows the orchestrator/worker distinction from `references/task-sizing.md`:

| Role | Tier |
|---|---|
| Orchestrating review — integrates findings across multiple axes or sub-passes | **Frontier** |
| Focused worker — one bounded review lane, single axis | **Balanced** |
| Mechanical leaf — deterministic checks with enumerable pass/fail criteria | **Fast** |

The rule of thumb: if this persona's verdict propagates to downstream decisions (the next agent acts on it), route **Frontier**. Single-axis, bounded-lane personas route **Balanced**; deterministic pass/fail checks route **Fast**. The tier mapping — `Fast = Haiku 4.5 · Balanced = Sonnet 4.6 · Frontier = Opus 4.7` — is canonical in `references/task-sizing.md`; a model bump is a one-line edit there.

## Length

Target 150–200 lines. Hard cap 250.

## Reject triggers specific to sub-agents

- `model:` field absent from frontmatter, or tier not justified against the orchestrator/worker distinction.
- Missing the "Review posture" section.
- Missing the "Context to load" section, or context loading happens after seeing the original work (this pollutes the independent view).
- The persona's lane overlaps another sub-agent's without an explicit `Out of scope` boundary.
- The persona is a thin wrapper around an existing skill rather than a genuine adversarial second pass.
- The persona's review verdict criteria reduce to "does this match my interpretation" — should be principle-grounded.

## See also

- `docs/skill-anatomy.md`, `docs/hook-anatomy.md`
- `docs/brief-template.md`
- `docs/authoring-learnings.md`

# Colleague-grade writing across Shaper artefacts

## Problem Statement
**How might we** make every project, milestone, and task Shaper emits read like a capable colleague wrote it — enforced at authoring time and again by an adversarial editor pass — without duplicating the ruleset or bloating the skills?

## Recommended Direction
Two artefacts, single source of truth, wired into the three skills you named.

**Phase 1 — authoring time.** Port your guidelines near-verbatim into `references/writing-rules.md` (matches the repo's port-first norm). Then add one citation line to each of `initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, and `delivery-shape` — placed at the artefact-template / synthesis step, the moment prose gets written, not buried in a "see also". Cite the way `delivery-shape` already cites `references/task-sizing.md`, so the pattern is familiar. The rules file routes its three sub-sections to the three skills: *Project Goals and OKRs* → initiative-shape, *Milestones and Deliverables* → roadmap-shape + delivery-shape, *Tasks/Stories/Bugs* → delivery-shape. Core Rules + Vocabulary + the Agent Review Checklist apply to all three.

**Phase 2 — refinement.** A new `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` persona, spec'd against `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`. Fresh-context adversarial pass that runs after an artefact-producing skill finishes, reads `references/writing-rules.md` *before* seeing the draft (so it forms an independent standard first), and returns `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject` plus line-level rewrites. Model tier **Balanced** (single bounded axis: prose quality; verdict doesn't fan out to multi-axis decisions). Its "Common rationalisations" section names what the original author skips — "the meaning's clear enough", nominalizations, blank tasks like "Investigate latency", milestones-as-labels. The persona's *checklist* and the skill's *inline rules cite the same file* — no second copy of the rules.

The shared file is the keystone: skills read it to write, the persona reads it to judge. Edit the rules once, both phases move.

## Key Assumptions to Validate
- [ ] **Citation placement lands at write-time, not as decoration.** Check: open each of the three skills, confirm the cite sits at the artefact-template/synthesis gate — test by reading the skill as an agent would and seeing if the rules arrive before the prose is composed.
- [ ] **The persona earns its existence** (sub-agent-anatomy reject trigger: "thin wrapper around an existing skill"). It must catch what the author structurally misses, not re-run the checklist the author already saw. Validate: the "Review posture" + "Common rationalisations" sections name author-specific blind spots, not generic style nits.
- [ ] **No lane overlap with `plan-review`.** plan-review owns scope/assumptions/operability of plans; writing-editor owns prose quality of tracker artefacts. The `Out of scope` section must draw that line explicitly or sub-agent-anatomy rejects it.
- [ ] **Three skills is the right scope.** Validate by checking whether design-doc prose noticeably degrades without it — if owners complain later, the cite is a one-line add.

## MVP Scope
**In:** `references/writing-rules.md` (full ported ruleset); three one-line citations; `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` (12-section persona per anatomy). **Out:** changing skill *workflow gates*, touching design-doc/idea-triage, building a hook (writing quality is judgement, not deterministic pass/fail — anatomy says embed-or-persona, not hook), and any auto-invocation wiring beyond what the skills already do for sub-agents.

## Not Doing (and Why)
- **A hook for writing checks** — `sub-agent-anatomy.md` is explicit: mechanical checks → hook, judgement → persona. Prose quality is judgement.
- **Folding into plan-review/execution-review** — you ruled it out, correctly: tracker-only initiatives and tasks never reach those reviews, so coverage would have holes.
- **Distilling the ruleset to principles** — full port keeps the vocabulary swaps (utilize→use, bottleneck→the specific shortage) that are the most copy-pasteable part, and matches the repo's port-first norm.
- **Touching design-doc/idea-triage now** — adding a cite later is one line; pulling scope back is not.

## Open Questions
- Does writing-editor fire automatically (referenced from the three skills' exit criteria) or on-demand? Auto matches your "after an agent finishes writing" intent — I'd wire the three skills' Exit-criteria to invoke it, mirroring how other skills hand off to sub-agents.
- One persona for all three artefact types, or does the milestone-vs-task rule split warrant section-scoped review prompts inside the one persona? (I lean: one persona, the rules file already segments by artefact.)

skills/exec-pickup/SKILL.md:16:Take a freshly-picked Linear issue all the way to a finished PR with a full review trail. On **non-Claude workers** (codex, kimi, or any worker without an Agent fan-out tool), run the personas inline-sequentially: load `personas/spec-compliance.md`, apply it to the diff, capture findings; then repeat for `personas/security-auditor.md`; then `personas/code-quality.md`.
skills/execution-review/SKILL.md:79:Each persona receives:
skills/execution-review/SKILL.md:84:Gather the raw finding triples from all three personas. Deduplicate on the match key `(file · defect_class · severity)` — if two personas surface the same triple, count it once. A finding that appears in two personas is not more severe; it is one finding with corroboration.
skills/execution-review/SKILL.md:120:| "Tests are passing so the spec compliance lens is unnecessary." | Tests verify what was tested, not what the AC required. The spec-compliance persona reads the AC directly against the diff — it cannot be substituted by a test run. |
skills/execution-review/SKILL.md:121:| "I already did a code review — running three personas is overkill." | A single reviewer has a single orientation. The three lenses are narrow by design; overlap is deduped, not double-counted. The cost is one extra dispatch; the benefit is two defect classes the single reviewer wasn't looking for. |
skills/execution-review/SKILL.md:122:| "Security is obvious — I'd have caught it manually." | Security findings have a strong availability bias: the obvious ones get caught; the structural ones (hardcoded secrets, string-concatenated queries) get normalised. The security-auditor persona's orientation is to find what the implementing agent normalised. |

1	# ADR 0003 — Persona contract and dispatch protocol
2	
3	- **Status:** Accepted
4	- **Date:** 2026-06-10
5	- **Serves:** Initiative D1 / KR2 — execution-review quality gate proven on seeded defects (N02).
6	- **Premise for:** Initiative C — thin supervisor that fans persona reviews out and merges their findings.
7	
8	## Context
9	
10	D1 builds a persona-driven review gate. To run the gate the same way across the worker surfaces this pack targets, two things must be pinned: how a persona is authored, and how it is dispatched. The surfaces differ materially:
11	
12	- **Claude Code** — has an `Agent` tool that fans out a prompt-file persona into a subagent, returning the subagent's final text.
13	- **codex / kimi / other non-Claude workers** — no equivalent fan-out tool; the worker is one process running one prompt at a time.
14	
15	A persona that hard-codes Claude-Code dispatch mechanics would strand the other workers and break initiative C's portability premise. A persona that hard-codes inline-sequential mechanics would forfeit the parallelism Claude Code already affords. The pack also needs a finding format the N01 grader can match on without parsing free prose; the grader looks for `file · defect class · severity` triples.
16	
17	Two contract shapes were considered:
18	
19	1. **Prompt-file personas + protocol in this ADR.** Persona prose lives in a plain `.md` file with frontmatter; dispatch rules live here, not in the personas. Portable; Claude-Code-native dispatch is preserved as the default branch.
20	2. **Claude-Code-native agent definitions** (`.claude/agents/*.md` with agent-tool metadata). Richer dispatch ergonomics on Claude Code but no usable form on codex/kimi; would require a second authoring track for non-Claude surfaces.
21	
22	Shape 1 is taken. The Agent tool can dispatch a plain prompt-file persona directly — its content is the prompt — so the Claude-Code ergonomics loss is small, and the non-Claude surface stays first-class.
23	
24	## Decision
25	
26	**Persona file format and location.** Personas live at `personas/<name>.md`. Each file is a self-contained prompt with YAML frontmatter:
27	
28	```yaml
29	---
30	name: <kebab-case>
31	description: <one-line role + when to invoke>
32	defect_classes: [<class>, <class>, ...]   # the classes this persona is responsible for catching
33	---
34	```
35	
36	The body is plain prose: role, what to look for, what to ignore, and the finding format (below). No dispatch mechanics in the persona — those are this ADR's job.
37	
38	**Structured finding format.** Each finding the persona emits is one line:
39	
40	```
41	<file>:<line?> · <defect_class> · <severity>
42	```
43	
44	`severity ∈ {critical, required, suggestion}`. `defect_class` is one of the persona's declared classes. Lines may be followed by indented free-prose context, but the leading triple is what the N01 grader matches on. The persona's prompt body restates this format verbatim.
45	
46	**Dispatch protocol.**
47	
48	- **Claude Code (default).** The supervisor reads `personas/<name>.md` and dispatches each persona via the `Agent` tool, passing the file content as the prompt and running personas in parallel (one `Agent` tool call per persona, batched in a single message). The Agent's returned text is the persona's findings list.
49	- **Non-Claude fallback (codex, kimi, others without fan-out).** The supervisor runs personas **inline-sequentially** in the same worker: for each persona, load `personas/<name>.md`, treat its body as a self-review instruction over the same diff, capture findings, then move to the next persona. The persona prose is identical to the Claude Code path; only the harness differs.
50	
51	Both branches return findings in the same triple format, so the N01 grader and the supervisor's merge step are dispatch-agnostic.
52	
53	## Consequences
54	
55	**Positive**
56	- One persona authoring surface serves both dispatch worlds; initiative C builds against a stable contract.
57	- The N01 grader and any future merger consume the same finding triples regardless of dispatch path.
58	- Adding a persona is a single-file change with no harness coupling.
59	
60	**Negative / costs**
61	- The non-Claude fallback gives up parallelism — review latency on codex/kimi scales linearly with persona count. Accepted because the alternative is a second authoring track.
62	- Finding-format discipline now lives in persona prose. Drift (a persona forgetting the triple) is silent until the grader sees it; N03 must verify the format holds during its iteration loop and add a lint check if drift recurs.
63	- Claude-Code-native agent affordances (named agent metadata, model pinning per agent) are not used. Re-evaluate if a future persona genuinely needs them.
64	
65	## Scope
66	
67	This ADR pins the contract only. N03 authors the personas against it and is the first consumer; the N01 fixture corpus and grader (already landed) are the acceptance harness. Any change to the finding triple or the dispatch branches requires a follow-up ADR.
68	
```

The writing-editor is a standalone auto-fired pass like `code-reviewer`, so `agents/` is its home; ADR 0003's dispatch protocol still governs *how* the three skills invoke it.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/.claude/plans/1-writing-editor-should-be-snug-nebula.md",
  "content": "# Task: Enforce colleague-grade writing in the three artefact-producing skills\n\n## Context\n\n`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, and `delivery-shape` emit the projects, milestones, and tasks the rest of Shaper acts on. Today each skill governs the *structure* of what it writes — fields, gates, templates — but nothing governs the *prose*. The result is artefacts that pass their verification rubric while still reading like a label list: nominalized goals (\"improve utilization\"), milestones used as headings (\"Frontend; Data\"), and tasks with no visible done-state (\"Investigate latency\").\n\nAnton supplied a writing ruleset for AI agents (actor-in-subject, concrete verbs, every task names a work product, known-context-first, specific vocabulary). This task lands that ruleset in two places: once at authoring time, so the skill writes well the first time, and once in a fresh-context editor pass, so a draft the author over-rates gets refined before a human sees it. The same rules file feeds both — skills read it to write, the editor reads it to judge — so the ruleset has one source.\n\nScope is the three named skills only. `design-doc` and `idea-triage` also emit prose, but adding a citation to them later costs one line; pulling scope back does not.\n\n## Outcome\n\nDone when a draft initiative, roadmap, or delivery plan produced by these skills is checked against the ruleset at authoring time, then refined by an automatic editor pass before it reaches the user — and the ruleset lives in exactly one file.\n\n## Why this shape\n\nThe repo already separates shared knowledge (`references/`) from review judgement (`agents/`). The two phases map onto that split with no new machinery:\n\n- **Phase 1 lives in `references/`.** Skills already cite reference files inline at the step where they need them — `delivery-shape:296` reads `references/task-sizing.md` mid-decompose. The writing rules join that pattern.\n- **Phase 2 lives in `agents/`.** `agents/code-reviewer/AGENT.md` is a standing persona that fires before a commit; the writing-editor is its sibling, firing before a draft reaches the user. `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` is its authoring spec. ADR 0003 governs how the skills dispatch it.\n\n## Subtasks\n\n### 1. Write `references/writing-rules.md`\n\nPort Anton's ruleset near-verbatim (the repo's port-first norm; see memory `port-first-from-predecessor-packs`). Keep his section order so skills can cite a section by name: Core Rules → Project Goals and OKRs → Milestones and Deliverables → Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors → Vocabulary → Agent Review Checklist. Preserve every bad/good example pair — the vocabulary swaps (utilize→use, bottleneck→the specific shortage) are the most reusable part. Match the house voice of the other `references/` files.\n\n**Done when:** `references/writing-rules.md` exists, carries all six sections with their examples intact, and reads in the same register as `references/task-sizing.md`.\n\n### 2. Cite the rules from each of the three skills at its write-step\n\nAdd one citation line per skill, placed where prose gets composed — not in a trailing \"Related\" list. Route each skill to the sections that govern its output:\n\n- `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Synthesize the draft* (~line 82) and the *Initiative description template* (~line 125). Sections: Core Rules + Project Goals and OKRs.\n- `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md` — at *Use the template below* (~line 190). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables.\n- `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Write the five-section node body* (~line 162) and Step 7 *Break each node into tasks* (~line 202). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables (node bodies) + Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors (tasks).\n\n**Done when:** each of the three skills cites `references/writing-rules.md` at its synthesis step, naming the sections that apply, in the same citation style the skill already uses for other references.\n\n### 3. Write `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`\n\nAuthor one persona against `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — all twelve sections in order. One persona reviews all three artefact types; the rules file already segments by artefact, so the persona selects the right section from what it is handed rather than splitting into three personas.\n\nKey fields and stances:\n- `model: Balanced` — a single bounded axis (prose quality), and its verdict does not fan out into multi-axis decisions.\n- `review_target: other` (artefact prose).\n- **Context to load:** `references/writing-rules.md`, read *before* the draft, so the editor forms the standard independently and then checks the draft against it.\n- **Review posture:** adversarial, no investment in the draft; carries the Agent Review Checklist as its lens.\n- **Common rationalisations:** name what the original author skips — \"the meaning is clear enough\", a nominalization that \"reads fine\", a milestone left as a label, a task like \"Investigate latency\" with no named output.\n- **Outputs:** verdict `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`, plus line-level rewrites for every flagged span.\n- **Out of scope:** prose quality only. Scope drift, operability, and one-way doors belong to `plan-review`; AC satisfaction belongs to `verify-implementation`. State this boundary explicitly — `sub-agent-anatomy.md` rejects a persona whose lane overlaps another without it.\n\n**Done when:** `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` passes every reject trigger in `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` (model tier justified, Review posture present, Context-to-load before the draft, explicit Out-of-scope boundary, not a thin wrapper over the rules file).\n\n### 4. Wire the editor to fire automatically from each skill\n\nAdd a step to each skill that dispatches the writing-editor over the freshly synthesized draft *before* the skill's user-confirmation / done gate, and loops back on a `reject`. Follow ADR 0003's dispatch protocol verbatim: on Claude Code, dispatch via the `Agent` tool with the persona file as the prompt; on a non-Claude worker, load the persona inline and self-review against the same draft. Place the step so the editor runs after the draft is complete but before the human sees it:\n\n- `initiative-shape` — between Step 6 (synthesize) and Step 7 (user confirmation).\n- `roadmap-shape` — before *Shape review* / *Verification*.\n- `delivery-shape` — at Step 9 (verify the plan), over the emitted node and task bodies.\n\nRecord the gate in each skill's Exit criteria: the skill is not complete until the editor returns `accept` or `accept with notes`.\n\n**Done when:** each skill names the editor dispatch as a step, cites ADR 0003 for the dispatch branches, places it before the user-facing hand-off, and lists editor `accept` in its Exit criteria.\n\n## Files\n\n- New: `references/writing-rules.md`, `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`\n- Edit: `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md`\n\n## Assumptions\n\n- The `agents/<name>/AGENT.md` form (not the lighter `personas/<name>.md` triple form) is correct, because the editor is a standing standalone pass like `code-reviewer`, not a member of `execution-review`'s graded fan-out.\n- Dispatching the editor as an automatic in-skill step (rather than a separate user-invoked review skill) matches \"fire automatically after the agent finishes writing\".\n\n## Key risks\n\n- **The editor degrades into a second copy of the rules.** Mitigation: the persona cites `references/writing-rules.md` and adds only the adversarial stance and the author-blind-spot list; if it restates the rules, `sub-agent-anatomy.md`'s thin-wrapper trigger should catch it in subtask 3.\n- **The auto-dispatch loop never terminates** if the editor keeps returning `reject`. Mitigation: cap at one refine loop, then surface remaining notes to the user as `accept with notes` rather than blocking.\n\n## Verification\n\n1. Read `references/writing-rules.md` against Anton's source ruleset — every section and example present, voice consistent with `references/task-sizing.md`.\n2. Grep each of the three skills for `references/writing-rules.md`; confirm the citation sits at the synthesis step and names the applicable sections.\n3. Check `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` against each reject trigger in `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`.\n4. End-to-end: run `initiative-shape` on a deliberately weak idea (\"improve API efficiency, utilize caching\"); confirm the draft cites the rules at synthesis and the editor pass rewrites the nominalizations before the user-confirmation gate.\n5. Repeat the end-to-end check for `delivery-shape` with a blank task (\"Investigate latency\") — the editor must flag the missing done-state.\n"
}

Replace nominalizations (\"implementation\",\"utilization\",\"optimization\") with \"implement\",\"use\",\"optimize\".\n- Make each goal/milestone/task produce a visible work product.\n- Start with known context, end with new/important information.\n- Cut filler, but keep assumptions, risks, constraints, dependencies, owners, dates, success criteria.\n- Write like a professional colleague; clear story over disconnected facts.\nProject Goals/OKRs: explain gap between current and desired state; SCQA logic internally (no labels); make success measurable.\nMilestones: summary achievements not labels; non-overlapping containers; ordered by sequence/structure/impact.\nTasks: every task needs a concrete \"done when\"; avoid blank tasks unless output is named; active voice.\nVocabulary: exact technical terms; \"essential/required\" over \"vital\"; name the specific shortage not \"bottleneck\"; \"plan/spec\" over \"blueprint\"; \"limit/maximum\" over \"ceiling\"; \"use\" over \"utilization\"; \"implement\" over \"implementation\"; \"communicate with/connect to/call\" over \"interface with\"; \"affect\" over \"impact\" (verb); delete \"basically\",\"actually\",\"quite\",\"it should be noted that\"; \"now\" over \"at this point in time\".\nAgent Review Checklist: moves problem→solution in clear order? subtasks explain how/why? pruning preserved assumptions/risks/dependencies/constraints/success criteria? every task's done-state visible and verifiable? important terms/conclusions where readers notice them? sounds like a capable colleague wrote it?\n\nNow read the draft to review:\n/Users/anton/.claude/plans/1-writing-editor-should-be-snug-nebula.md\n\nReturn your review in this exact shape:\n1. Be precise enough that the edits can be applied verbatim — quote uniquely matchable strings.\n3. Run the Agent Review Checklist as 6 yes/no lines at the end.\n\nDo not edit any files. Return only the review."

This is a prose review task, not an implementation task. I'll review the document against the ruleset and return the review directly.

# Writing Review: `1-writing-editor-should-be-snug-nebula.md`

## 1. Verdict: `accept with notes`

The draft is strong — active voice throughout, every subtask carries a concrete "Done when," and assumptions/risks/dependencies are preserved. A handful of spans trip the vocabulary and nominalization rules.

## 2. Flagged spans

- Original: `nothing governs the *prose*` → Rewrite: `no rule governs the *prose*` — Vocabulary/active subject: name the actor (a rule), not a vague "nothing governs."

- Original: `nominalized goals ("improve utilization")` → Rewrite: `nominalized goals ("improve utilization" instead of "use more of X")` — Concrete verbs: the example flags a nominalization but does not show the concrete-verb fix, weakening it as an illustration (minor; optional).

- Original: `once at authoring time, so the skill writes well the first time, and once in a fresh-context editor pass` → Rewrite: keep — clear story, no change needed.

- Original: `pulling scope back does not` → Rewrite: `pulling scope back later does not` — Clarity: parallel with the prior clause's implied "later"; reads as a colleague intended.

- Original: `The two phases map onto that split with no new machinery` → Rewrite: keep, but consider `map onto that split without adding new machinery` — Nominalization-adjacent: "machinery" is metaphor, acceptable in house voice; flag only if strict.

- Original: `the vocabulary swaps (utilize→use, bottleneck→the specific shortage)` → Rewrite: keep — correctly names the specific shortage rather than "bottleneck," modeling the rule.

- Original: `Author one persona against` → Rewrite: `Write one persona against` — Concrete verbs: "Author" as a verb is borderline; "Write" matches the surrounding imperative voice ("Write `references/...`", "Add one citation line"). Consistency.

- Original: `name what the original author skips` → Rewrite: keep — active, concrete.

- Original: `gets refined before a human sees it` and `before it reaches the user` (Outcome, line 13) → Rewrite: keep — but note the doc alternates "the user" / "a human" / "the human" for the same actor (lines 7, 13, 57, 65). Pick one term throughout — exact-term consistency. Suggest "the user."

- Original: `a draft the author over-rates` (line 7) and `a draft the author over-rates gets refined` → Rewrite: keep — vivid, colleague register.

- Original: `End-to-end: run \`initiative-shape\` on a deliberately weak idea` → Rewrite: keep — names a visible, verifiable check.

- Original: `the editor must flag the missing done-state` → Rewrite: keep — concrete success criterion.

## 3. Agent Review Checklist

1. Moves problem→solution in clear order? — Yes. Context names the gap, Outcome states the target, Why-this-shape justifies, Subtasks deliver.
2. Subtasks explain how/why? — Yes. Each gives placement, sections, and rationale (e.g., one persona because the rules file segments by artefact).
3. Pruning preserved assumptions/risks/dependencies/constraints/success criteria? — Yes. Dedicated Assumptions, Key risks, and per-subtask "Done when" plus a Verification section.
4. Every task's done-state visible and verifiable? — Yes. All four subtasks carry a "Done when" naming a checkable artefact or condition.
5. Important terms/conclusions where readers notice them? — Mostly. Bolded lead-ins and "Done when" placement work well; only weakness is the inconsistent user/human/the-human term for the same actor.
6. Sounds like a capable colleague wrote it? — Yes. Direct, principle-named, no filler.

18	          "body": "lets add design doc into this scope\n\ni've updated the guidelines, see below. if there are things that should be removed from the writing guidelines because they duplicate what is already in the skill pack then make note of that.\n\nWriting Guidelines for AI Agents\nUse these rules for software engineering and product management writing.\nCore Rules\nPut the actor in the subject and the action in the verb. Prefer \"The API caches responses\" over \"Caching is implemented by the API.\"\nUse concrete verbs. Replace nominalizations such as \"implementation,\" \"utilization,\" and \"optimization\" with \"implement,\" \"use,\" and \"optimize.\"\nMake each goal, milestone, or task produce a visible work product: a shipped change, design doc, verified fix, report, migration, test, or decision.\nStart with known context and end with the new or important information.\nCut filler, but keep assumptions, risks, constraints, dependencies, owners, dates, and success criteria.\nWrite like a professional colleague. Prefer a clear story over a list of disconnected facts.\nProject Goals and OKRs\nExplain the gap between the current state, the desired state, and the cost of staying where you are.\nUse Situation, Complication, Question, Answer logic internally, but do not use those labels as headings.\nMake success measurable.\nBad:\n\"Improve the efficiency of our API and utilize better caching.\"\nGood:\n\"Our API serves 1k RPS today. The Q3 launch requires 10k RPS, which will exceed current capacity. We will re-architect the load balancer to support 15k RPS.\"\nBad:\n\"Significantly improve dashboard speed.\"\nGood:\n\"Reduce P95 dashboard load time from 4.5s to under 800ms using Edge Workers.\"\nDesign Docs\nProblem: Define the current state and the cost of the status quo.\nContext: Anchor the reader in stable facts before introducing the change or conflict.\nConstraints: Group non-functional requirements by structure or impact.\nAlternatives: Compare distinct options and state the practical effect of each one.\nRecommended approach: Start with what the team already knows, then introduce the proposed change.\nOperability plan: Define verifiable rollback gates, monitoring signals, and ownership.\nMilestones and Deliverables\nUse milestones as summary achievements, not labels such as \"Phase 1\" or \"Research.\"\nGroup work into non-overlapping containers.\nOrder groups by sequence, system structure, or impact.\nBad:\n\"Frontend; Data; Implementation.\"\nGood:\n\"Built responsive React components; Migrated user records to a sharded Postgres cluster.\"\nBad:\n\"Security; Authentication; Login Page.\"\nGood:\n\"Identity management; Data encryption at rest.\"\nTasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors\nEvery task needs a concrete \"done when\" state.\nAvoid blank tasks such as \"Investigate latency\" unless the output is named.\nUse active voice.\nBad:\n\"Perform an investigation into the latency situation.\"\nGood:\n\"Done when: The report identifies the three highest-latency queries in the /orders endpoint.\"\nBad:\n\"As a user, I want the utilization of an automated system.\"\nGood:\n\"As a user, I want to reset my password through an email link so I can regain access in under 2 minutes.\"\nVocabulary\nUse exact technical terms when they are accurate. Replace metaphors only when they hide the cause.\nPrefer \"essential\" or \"required\" over vague intensifiers such as \"vital.\"\nReplace \"bottleneck\" with the specific shortage, delay, queue, lock, or capacity limit when known.\nPrefer \"plan\" or \"spec\" over \"blueprint\" unless discussing an actual final design.\nPrefer \"limit\" or \"maximum\" over \"ceiling.\"\nPrefer \"use\" over \"utilization.\"\nPrefer \"implement\" over \"implementation\" when describing action.\nPrefer \"communicate with,\" \"connect to,\" or \"call\" over \"interface with.\"\nPrefer \"affect\" over \"impact\" as a verb.\nDelete \"basically,\" \"actually,\" \"quite,\" \"it should be noted that,\" and similar throat-clearing.\nReplace \"at this point in time\" with \"now.\"\nAgent Review Checklist\nBefore returning an artifact, check:\nDoes it move from problem to solution in a clear order?\nDo subtasks explain how to achieve the milestone or why the milestone matters?\nDid pruning preserve assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria?\nIs every task's done state visible and verifiable?\nDo important terms or conclusions appear where readers will notice them?\nDoes it sound like a capable colleague wrote it?",

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/.claude/plans/1-writing-editor-should-be-snug-nebula.md",
  "content": "# Task: Enforce colleague-grade writing in the four artefact-producing skills\n\n## Context\n\n`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, `delivery-shape`, and `design-doc` emit the projects, milestones, tasks, and design documents the rest of Shaper acts on. Today each skill governs the *structure* of what it writes — fields, gates, templates — but no rule governs the *prose*. The result is artefacts that pass their verification rubric while still reading like a label list: nominalized goals (\"improve utilization\"), milestones used as headings (\"Frontend; Data\"), and tasks with no visible done-state (\"Investigate latency\").\n\nAnton supplied a writing ruleset for AI agents (actor-in-subject, concrete verbs, every task names a work product, known-context-first, specific vocabulary). This task lands that ruleset in two places: once at authoring time, so the skill writes well the first time, and once in a fresh-context editor pass, so a draft the author over-rates gets refined before the user sees it. The same rules file feeds both — skills read it to write, the editor reads it to judge — so the ruleset has one source.\n\nScope is these four skills. `idea-triage` also emits prose, but adding a citation to it later costs one line; pulling scope back later does not.\n\n## Outcome\n\nDone when a draft initiative, roadmap, delivery plan, or design doc produced by these skills is checked against the ruleset at authoring time, then refined by an automatic editor pass before it reaches the user — and the ruleset lives in exactly one file, with no rule that merely restates a gate a skill already enforces.\n\n## Why this shape\n\nThe repo already separates shared knowledge (`references/`) from review judgement (`agents/`). The two phases map onto that split without adding new machinery:\n\n- **Phase 1 lives in `references/`.** Skills already cite reference files inline at the step where they need them — `delivery-shape:296` reads `references/task-sizing.md` mid-decompose. The writing rules join that pattern.\n- **Phase 2 lives in `agents/`.** `agents/code-reviewer/AGENT.md` is a standing persona that fires before a commit; the writing-editor is its sibling, firing before a draft reaches the user. `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` is its authoring spec. ADR 0003 governs how the skills dispatch it.\n\n## Subtasks\n\n### 1. Write `references/writing-rules.md`\n\nPort Anton's updated ruleset near-verbatim (the repo's port-first norm; see memory `port-first-from-predecessor-packs`). Keep his section order so skills can cite a section by name: Core Rules → Project Goals and OKRs → Design Docs → Milestones and Deliverables → Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors → Vocabulary → Agent Review Checklist. Preserve every bad/good example pair — the vocabulary swaps (utilize→use, bottleneck→the specific shortage) are the most reusable part. Match the house voice of the other `references/` files.\n\nTwo additions from Anton's revised ruleset must land:\n- Project Goals and OKRs: the gap statement now names three things — current state, desired state, **and the cost of staying where you are.**\n- A new **Design Docs** section, and the milestone rule's new \"not labels such as 'Phase 1' or 'Research'\" examples.\n\n**Done when:** `references/writing-rules.md` exists, carries all seven sections with their examples intact (including the two additions above), and reads in the same register as `references/task-sizing.md`.\n\n### 2. Reconcile the rules against gates the skills already enforce\n\nAnton's request: flag any guideline rule that duplicates structure a skill already mandates, so the rules file governs *prose* and the skill keeps owning *structure*. The clearest overlap is the new **Design Docs** section against `design-doc`'s existing gated steps:\n\n- \"Problem: current state and cost of status quo\" ≈ `design-doc` Step 3 (Problem gate).\n- \"Constraints: group NFRs by structure/impact\" ≈ Step 4 + `references/nfr-categories.md`.\n- \"Alternatives: compare distinct options, state each one's effect\" ≈ Step 5.\n- \"Recommended approach\" ≈ Step 6. \"Operability plan: rollback gates, monitoring, ownership\" ≈ Step 8 (Operability gate).\n\nResolution: the writing-rules Design Docs section keeps only the **prose** guidance within each section (anchor the reader in stable facts before the change; compare options by practical effect; lead with what the team already knows). It must not re-mandate which sections exist or their order — `design-doc` owns that. Apply the same test to the other sections and record any further cuts.\n\n**Done when:** the Design Docs section of `references/writing-rules.md` carries no structural mandate that `design-doc` already gates, and the plan's author has recorded (in the file or commit message) each rule dropped as a duplicate and why.\n\n### 3. Cite the rules from each of the four skills at its write-step\n\nAdd one citation line per skill, placed where prose gets composed — not in a trailing \"Related\" list. Route each skill to the sections that govern its output:\n\n- `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Synthesize the draft* (~line 82) and the *Initiative description template* (~line 125). Sections: Core Rules + Project Goals and OKRs.\n- `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md` — at *Use the template below* (~line 190). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables.\n- `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Write the five-section node body* (~line 162) and Step 7 *Break each node into tasks* (~line 202). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables (node bodies) + Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors (tasks).\n- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — at the *Artefact template* (~line 86), covering synthesis Steps 3–9. Sections: Core Rules + Design Docs.\n\n**Done when:** each of the four skills cites `references/writing-rules.md` at its synthesis step, naming the sections that apply, in the same citation style the skill already uses for other references.\n\n### 4. Write `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`\n\nWrite one persona against `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — all twelve sections in order. One persona reviews all four artefact types; the rules file already segments by artefact, so the persona selects the right section from what it is handed rather than splitting into four personas.\n\nKey fields and stances:\n- `model: Balanced` — a single bounded axis (prose quality), and its verdict does not fan out into multi-axis decisions.\n- `review_target: other` (artefact prose).\n- **Context to load:** `references/writing-rules.md`, read *before* the draft, so the editor forms the standard independently and then checks the draft against it.\n- **Review posture:** adversarial, no investment in the draft; carries the Agent Review Checklist as its lens.\n- **Common rationalisations:** name what the original author skips — \"the meaning is clear enough\", a nominalization that \"reads fine\", a milestone left as a label, a task like \"Investigate latency\" with no named output.\n- **Outputs:** verdict `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`, plus line-level rewrites for every flagged span.\n- **Out of scope:** prose quality only. Scope drift, operability, and one-way doors belong to `plan-review`; AC satisfaction belongs to `verify-implementation`. State this boundary explicitly — `sub-agent-anatomy.md` rejects a persona whose lane overlaps another without it.\n\n**Done when:** `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` passes every reject trigger in `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` (model tier justified, Review posture present, Context-to-load before the draft, explicit Out-of-scope boundary, not a thin wrapper over the rules file).\n\n### 5. Wire the editor to fire automatically from each skill\n\nAdd a step to each skill that dispatches the writing-editor over the freshly synthesized draft *before* the skill's user-confirmation / done gate, and loops back on a `reject`. Follow ADR 0003's dispatch protocol verbatim: on Claude Code, dispatch via the `Agent` tool with the persona file as the prompt; on a non-Claude worker, load the persona inline and self-review against the same draft. Place the step so the editor runs after the draft is complete but before the user sees it:\n\n- `initiative-shape` — between Step 6 (synthesize) and Step 7 (user confirmation).\n- `roadmap-shape` — before *Shape review* / *Verification*.\n- `delivery-shape` — at Step 9 (verify the plan), over the emitted node and task bodies.\n- `design-doc` — at Step 10 (Review hand-off), before the doc goes to `plan-review`.\n\nRecord the gate in each skill's Exit criteria: the skill is not complete until the editor returns `accept` or `accept with notes`.\n\n**Done when:** each skill names the editor dispatch as a step, cites ADR 0003 for the dispatch branches, places it before the user-facing hand-off, and lists editor `accept` in its Exit criteria.\n\n## Files\n\n- New: `references/writing-rules.md`, `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`\n- Edit: `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md`\n\n## Assumptions\n\n- The `agents/<name>/AGENT.md` form (not the lighter `personas/<name>.md` triple form) is correct, because the editor is a standing standalone pass like `code-reviewer`, not a member of `execution-review`'s graded fan-out.\n- Dispatching the editor as an automatic in-skill step (rather than a separate user-invoked review skill) matches \"fire automatically after the agent finishes writing\".\n\n## Key risks\n\n- **The editor degrades into a second copy of the rules.** Mitigation: the persona cites `references/writing-rules.md` and adds only the adversarial stance and the author-blind-spot list; if it restates the rules, `sub-agent-anatomy.md`'s thin-wrapper trigger should catch it in subtask 4.\n- **The Design Docs section re-mandates `design-doc`'s structure.** Mitigation: subtask 2 strips structural mandates and keeps prose guidance only.\n- **The auto-dispatch loop never terminates** if the editor keeps returning `reject`. Mitigation: cap at one refine loop, then surface remaining notes to the user as `accept with notes` rather than blocking.\n\n## Verification\n\n1. Read `references/writing-rules.md` against Anton's revised source ruleset — all seven sections and examples present, including the cost-of-status-quo gap statement and the Design Docs section; voice consistent with `references/task-sizing.md`.\n2. Confirm subtask 2's de-duplication: the Design Docs section names no section/order requirement that `design-doc` Steps 3–8 already gate, and the dropped rules are recorded.\n3. Grep each of the four skills for `references/writing-rules.md`; confirm the citation sits at the synthesis step and names the applicable sections.\n4. Check `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` against each reject trigger in `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`.\n5. End-to-end: run `initiative-shape` on a deliberately weak idea (\"improve API efficiency, utilize caching\"); confirm the draft cites the rules at synthesis and the editor pass rewrites the nominalizations before the user-confirmation gate.\n6. Repeat the end-to-end check for `delivery-shape` with a blank task (\"Investigate latency\") — the editor must flag the missing done-state — and for `design-doc`, confirming the editor refines prose without duplicating the structural gates.\n"
}

## Approved Plan (edited by user):
# Task: Enforce colleague-grade writing in the four artefact-producing skills

## Context

`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, `delivery-shape`, and `design-doc` emit the projects, milestones, tasks, and design documents the rest of Shaper acts on. Today each skill governs the *structure* of what it writes — fields, gates, templates — but no rule governs the *prose*. The result is artefacts that pass their verification rubric while still reading like a label list: nominalized goals ("improve utilization"), milestones used as headings ("Frontend; Data"), and tasks with no visible done-state ("Investigate latency").

Anton supplied a writing ruleset for AI agents (actor-in-subject, concrete verbs, every task names a work product, known-context-first, specific vocabulary). This task lands that ruleset in two places: once at authoring time, so the skill writes well the first time, and once in a fresh-context editor pass, so a draft the author over-rates gets refined before the user sees it. The same rules file feeds both — skills read it to write, the editor reads it to judge — so the ruleset has one source.

Scope is these four skills. `idea-triage` also emits prose, but adding a citation to it later costs one line; pulling scope back later does not.

## Outcome

Done when a draft initiative, roadmap, delivery plan, or design doc produced by these skills is checked against the ruleset at authoring time, then refined by an automatic editor pass before it reaches the user — and the ruleset lives in exactly one file, with no rule that merely restates a gate a skill already enforces.

## Why this shape

The repo already separates shared knowledge (`references/`) from review judgement (`agents/`). The two phases map onto that split without adding new machinery:

- **Phase 1 lives in `references/`.** Skills already cite reference files inline at the step where they need them — `delivery-shape:296` reads `references/task-sizing.md` mid-decompose. The writing rules join that pattern.
- **Phase 2 lives in `agents/`.** `agents/code-reviewer/AGENT.md` is a standing persona that fires before a commit; the writing-editor is its sibling, firing before a draft reaches the user. `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` is its authoring spec. ADR 0003 governs how the skills dispatch it.

## Subtasks

### 1. Write `references/writing-rules.md`

Port Anton's updated ruleset near-verbatim (the repo's port-first norm; see memory `port-first-from-predecessor-packs`). Keep his section order so skills can cite a section by name: Core Rules → Project Goals and OKRs → Design Docs → Milestones and Deliverables → Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors → Vocabulary → Agent Review Checklist. Preserve every bad/good example pair — the vocabulary swaps (utilize→use, bottleneck→the specific shortage) are the most reusable part. Match the house voice of the other `references/` files.

Two additions from Anton's revised ruleset must land:
- Project Goals and OKRs: the gap statement now names three things — current state, desired state, **and the cost of staying where you are.**
- A new **Design Docs** section, and the milestone rule's new "not labels such as 'Phase 1' or 'Research'" examples.

**Done when:** `references/writing-rules.md` exists, carries all seven sections with their examples intact (including the two additions above), and reads in the same register as `references/task-sizing.md`.

### 2. Reconcile the rules against gates the skills already enforce

Anton's request: flag any guideline rule that duplicates structure a skill already mandates, so the rules file governs *prose* and the skill keeps owning *structure*. The clearest overlap is the new **Design Docs** section against `design-doc`'s existing gated steps:

- "Problem: current state and cost of status quo" ≈ `design-doc` Step 3 (Problem gate).
- "Constraints: group NFRs by structure/impact" ≈ Step 4 + `references/nfr-categories.md`.
- "Alternatives: compare distinct options, state each one's effect" ≈ Step 5.
- "Recommended approach" ≈ Step 6. "Operability plan: rollback gates, monitoring, ownership" ≈ Step 8 (Operability gate).

Resolution: the writing-rules Design Docs section keeps only the **prose** guidance within each section (anchor the reader in stable facts before the change; compare options by practical effect; lead with what the team already knows). It must not re-mandate which sections exist or their order — `design-doc` owns that. Apply the same test to the other sections and record any further cuts.

**Done when:** the Design Docs section of `references/writing-rules.md` carries no structural mandate that `design-doc` already gates, and the plan's author has recorded (in the file or commit message) each rule dropped as a duplicate and why.

### 3. Cite the rules from each of the four skills at its write-step

Add one citation line per skill, placed where prose gets composed — not in a trailing "Related" list. Route each skill to the sections that govern its output:

- `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Synthesize the draft* (~line 82) and the *Initiative description template* (~line 125). Sections: Core Rules + Project Goals and OKRs.
- `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md` — at *Use the template below* (~line 190). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables.
- `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md` — Step 6 *Write the five-section node body* (~line 162) and Step 7 *Break each node into tasks* (~line 202). Sections: Core Rules + Milestones and Deliverables (node bodies) + Tasks, Stories, Bugs, and Refactors (tasks).
- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — at the *Artefact template* (~line 86), covering synthesis Steps 3–9. Sections: Core Rules + Design Docs.

**Done when:** each of the four skills cites `references/writing-rules.md` at its synthesis step, naming the sections that apply, in the same citation style the skill already uses for other references.

### 4. Write `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`

Write one persona against `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — all twelve sections in order. One persona reviews all four artefact types; the rules file already segments by artefact, so the persona selects the right section from what it is handed rather than splitting into four personas.

Key fields and stances:
- `model: Balanced` — a single bounded axis (prose quality), and its verdict does not fan out into multi-axis decisions.
- `review_target: other` (artefact prose).
- **Context to load:** `references/writing-rules.md`, read *before* the draft, so the editor forms the standard independently and then checks the draft against it.
- **Review posture:** adversarial, no investment in the draft; carries the Agent Review Checklist as its lens.
- **Common rationalisations:** name what the original author skips — "the meaning is clear enough", a nominalization that "reads fine", a milestone left as a label, a task like "Investigate latency" with no named output.
- **Outputs:** verdict `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`, plus line-level rewrites for every flagged span.
- **Out of scope:** prose quality only. Scope drift, operability, and one-way doors belong to `plan-review`; AC satisfaction belongs to `verify-implementation`. State this boundary explicitly — `sub-agent-anatomy.md` rejects a persona whose lane overlaps another without it.

**Done when:** `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` passes every reject trigger in `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` (model tier justified, Review posture present, Context-to-load before the draft, explicit Out-of-scope boundary, not a thin wrapper over the rules file).

### 5. Wire the editor to fire automatically from each skill

Add a step to each skill that dispatches the writing-editor over the freshly synthesized draft *before* the skill's user-confirmation / done gate, and loops back on a `reject`. Follow ADR 0003's dispatch protocol verbatim: on Claude Code, dispatch via the `Agent` tool with the persona file as the prompt; on a non-Claude worker, load the persona inline and self-review against the same draft. Place the step so the editor runs after the draft is complete but before the user sees it:

- `initiative-shape` — between Step 6 (synthesize) and Step 7 (user confirmation).
- `roadmap-shape` — before *Shape review* / *Verification*.
- `delivery-shape` — at Step 9 (verify the plan), over the emitted node and task bodies.
- `design-doc` — at Step 10 (Review hand-off), before the doc goes to `plan-review`.

Record the gate in each skill's Exit criteria: the skill is not complete until the editor returns `accept` or `accept with notes`.

**Done when:** each skill names the editor dispatch as a step, cites ADR 0003 for the dispatch branches, places it before the user-facing hand-off, and lists editor `accept` in its Exit criteria.

## Files

- New: `references/writing-rules.md`, `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`
- Edit: `skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md`, `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md`

## Assumptions

- The `agents/<name>/AGENT.md` form (not the lighter `personas/<name>.md` triple form) is correct, because the editor is a standing standalone pass like `code-reviewer`, not a member of `execution-review`'s graded fan-out.
- Dispatching the editor as an automatic in-skill step (rather than a separate user-invoked review skill) matches "fire automatically after the agent finishes writing".

## Key risks

- **The editor degrades into a second copy of the rules.** Mitigation: the persona cites `references/writing-rules.md` and adds only the adversarial stance and the author-blind-spot list; if it restates the rules, `sub-agent-anatomy.md`'s thin-wrapper trigger should catch it in subtask 4.
- **The Design Docs section re-mandates `design-doc`'s structure.** Mitigation: subtask 2 strips structural mandates and keeps prose guidance only.
- **The auto-dispatch loop never terminates** if the editor keeps returning `reject`. Mitigation: cap at one refine loop, then surface remaining notes to the user as `accept with notes` rather than blocking.

2. Confirm subtask 2's de-duplication: the Design Docs section names no section/order requirement that `design-doc` Steps 3–8 already gate, and the dropped rules are recorded.

5. End-to-end: run `initiative-shape` on a deliberately weak idea ("improve API efficiency, utilize caching"); confirm the draft cites the rules at synthesis and the editor pass rewrites the nominalizations before the user-confirmation gate.
6. Repeat the end-to-end check for `delivery-shape` with a blank task ("Investigate latency") — the editor must flag the missing done-state — and for `design-doc`, confirming the editor refines prose without duplicating the structural gates.

===== task-sizing.md (voice sample) =====
---
name: task-sizing
description: >
  Risk-weighted model-routing rubric — five axes scored Low/Med/High that route a
  single task to a model tier and a review-attention flag at planning time. Not effort
  estimation; a second lens on the verification-granularity Size check.
type: reference
cited_by:
  - skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md
  - skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md
---

# Task sizing — the 5-axis routing rubric

This rubric routes a single task to a **model tier** and a **review-attention flag** at planning time. It is risk-weighted *routing*, not effort estimation: the output is "which model should run this, and how much review does it warrant," never "how long will this take." You score five axes Low/Med/High, the stakes axis sets a default direction, the other axes adjust it, and a tier and a flag fall out.

Routing is not cascading. **Routing** is planning-time pre-classification — one model is chosen per task *before* it runs. **Cascading** is runtime escalation — start on a cheap model and retry on a bigger one mid-execution when the cheap one fails. This rubric only does routing; cascading is a named, separate, out-of-scope layer (see *What this is not for*).

**Reconciliation with eng-agentic P8.** P8 says effort is measured in slices and gates, not calendar time, and warns against importing a "how long will this take" frame. A sizing rubric could look like it smuggles effort estimation back in. It does not: it emits a capability tier keyed to *risk*, never a duration or a size. It is orthogonal to — and applied *after* — the slice-based Size check performed during at-pickup task breakdown. Two lenses on the same already-sized slice: one asks "is this one verifiable change" (Size), the other asks "what does it cost to get it wrong, and what capability does it need" (this rubric). Neither uses calendar time.

## The 5 active axes

Score each axis Low / Med / High by **routing pressure** — High means "argues for a more capable model." Four axes read naturally (more demanding = higher pressure). **Specification completeness runs inverted**: a *less* complete spec is *higher* pressure, because the model must infer intent rather than follow it.

| Axis | Low pressure | Medium | High pressure |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Reasoning complexity** | mechanical edit, lookup, rename, reformat | bounded logic following a known pattern | open-ended design, cross-cutting synthesis, subtle algorithmic correctness |
| **Specification completeness** *(inverted)* | fully specified — concrete acceptance criteria, named files, no judgment left | mostly specified, a few gaps to fill | sparse or ambiguous — intent must be inferred, acceptance criteria absent |
| **Hallucination sensitivity** | self-contained; no external API or library surface | familiar, stable APIs the model knows well | unfamiliar or fast-moving APIs, version-specific behaviour, high fabricated-signature risk |
| **Stakes / reversibility** | trivially reversible two-way door — one file, easy revert, low blast radius | reversible with effort; bounded blast radius | one-way door — schema migration, public API, auth, production data, security |

===== initiative-shape 80-130 =====
- **Kill condition:** what observable state says the bet did not work and we should stop? Examples: "If [KR] fails for [N] consecutive cycles", "If we ship [X] and [baseline metric] does not move", or "If the research establishes [Y]."

### 6. Synthesize the draft

Draft the six fields using the template below. The Goal is the Objective. Each KR headline is the observable claim; do not add a separate `state:` field.

Keep KR language concrete. Avoid "improve", "better", "enhance", and arbitrary "run N times" thresholds unless paired with a correctness or quality dimension.

Use `references/kr-quality-templates.md` only when you need help choosing strong KR dimensions, roles, or evidence patterns. Do not add rubric/audit annotations to the user-facing shape.

Optional note: include **Dominant model tier** when task routing matters. Use `Fast` for mostly mechanical rewrites, `Balanced` for bounded but non-trivial reasoning, and `Frontier` for one-way doors such as schema migrations, public APIs, auth, production data, or orchestrator-level decomposition. See `references/task-sizing.md`.

Present the draft. Do not record or emit it yet.

### 7. Gate: user confirmation

Ask: "Does this capture the initiative correctly? Any changes to the Objective, Key results, kill condition, or project type before I create the project?"

Do not proceed until the user confirms.

### 8. Gate: verification rubric — 11 cross-cutting rules

Grade the confirmed draft against these checks. Each one must pass before capture. If a check fails, repair the draft in Step 6 and re-confirm only the changed parts with the user.

| #   | Rule | Pass condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1   | Goal is outcome-shaped | Names who is affected and what should be true for them; does not describe a solution to build |
| 2   | Project type fits | One of the six project types is selected, and the KRs fit that type's default mix closely enough to be useful |
| 3   | KRs are few and distinct | 3-5 KRs, each measuring a different important dimension of success |
| 4   | KRs are observable | Each KR has a real baseline, target, measurement window, and evidence source |
| 5   | KRs are not fake progress | No KR is vague, degenerate, a restatement of the Goal, or an arbitrary output count |
| 6   | KR roles are clear | Each KR is marked as a `bet`, `brake`, or `foundation`, so downstream deliverables can be sequenced correctly |
| 7   | Scope has an off-ramp | Appetite is expressed as `~N issues`, and the kill condition names an observable stop state |

If every KR is tagged `bet`, ask whether a brake or foundation KR is missing. Do not force one of each role; some shapes are honestly 2 bets + 1 foundation, or 1 bet + 2 brakes.

Report only the failing rule numbers and the shortest useful reason. Do not expose internal rubric vocabulary in the final initiative shape.

### 9. Capture or hand off the shape

Take exactly one branch:

- **Tracker binding installed**: follow the loaded tracker-capture procedure. Pass the confirmed six-field shape as the `description`, the goal/problem label as the name, and the planning/not-yet-in-cycle state. Confirm with the locator returned by that procedure.
- **No tracker binding**: emit the confirmed shape as the markdown block below. Do not attempt a tracker call.

## Initiative description template

The shape reads as a product OKR — a non-pack-author should be able to grade each KR in 30 seconds. Each KR's headline *is* the observable claim (no separate `state:` field). The four sub-fields use customer/business friendly names.

```markdown
**Goal:** For [who], we want to [solve problem / achieve outcome].
```

{

  "content": "---\nname: writing-rules\ndescription: >\n  Prose ruleset for the artefacts Shaper writes — initiatives, OKRs, milestones, tasks,\n  and design docs. Governs how the words read, not which fields exist. Cited at each\n  skill's synthesis step and carried as the lens for the writing-editor persona.\ntype: reference\ncited_by:\n  - skills/initiative-shape/SKILL.md\n  - skills/roadmap-shape/SKILL.md\n  - skills/delivery-shape/SKILL.md\n  - skills/design-doc/SKILL.md\n  - agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md\n---\n\n# Writing rules\n\nUse these rules for the software-engineering and product-management writing Shaper produces. They govern **prose** — the sentences inside a goal, a milestone, a task, a design-doc section. They do not govern **structure**: which fields a skill emits and in what order is the skill's job, not this file's. Where a rule below would re-mandate a section a skill already gates, the rule keeps only its prose guidance and drops the structural part.\n\n## Core rules\n\n- Put the actor in the subject and the action in the verb. Prefer \"The API caches responses\" over \"Caching is implemented by the API.\"\n- Use concrete verbs. Replace nominalizations such as \"implementation,\" \"utilization,\" and \"optimization\" with \"implement,\" \"use,\" and \"optimize.\"\n- Make each goal, milestone, or task produce a visible work product: a shipped change, design doc, verified fix, report, migration, test, or decision.\n- Start with known context and end with the new or important information.\n- Cut filler, but keep assumptions, risks, constraints, dependencies, owners, dates, and success criteria.\n- Write like a professional colleague. Prefer a clear story over a list of disconnected facts.\n\n## Project goals and OKRs\n\n- Explain the gap between the current state, the desired state, and the cost of staying where you are.\n- Use Situation, Complication, Question, Answer logic internally, but do not use those labels as headings.\n- Make success measurable.\n\nBad: \"Improve the efficiency of our API and utilize better caching.\"\nGood: \"Our API serves 1k RPS today. The Q3 launch requires 10k RPS, which will exceed current capacity. We will re-architect the load balancer to support 15k RPS.\"\n\nBad: \"Significantly improve dashboard speed.\"\nGood: \"Reduce P95 dashboard load time from 4.5s to under 800ms using Edge Workers.\"\n\n## Design docs\n\nThis section governs the prose inside a design doc. Which sections a design doc contains, and their order, is owned by the `design-doc` skill's gates — do not restate that here.\n\n- Problem: name the current state and the cost of the status quo, not just the desired feature.\n- Context: anchor the reader in stable facts before introducing the change or conflict.\n- Constraints: state each non-functional requirement in concrete, checkable terms.\n- Alternatives: compare distinct options and state the practical effect of each one, not a list of properties.\n- Recommended approach: start with what the team already knows, then introduce the proposed change.\n- Operability: state rollback gates, monitoring signals, and ownership as verifiable conditions, not intentions.\n\n## Milestones and deliverables\n\n- Use milestones as summary achievements, not labels such as \"Phase 1\" or \"Research.\"\n- Group work into non-overlapping containers.\n- Order groups by sequence, system structure, or impact.\n\nBad: \"Frontend; Data; Implementation.\"\nGood: \"Built responsive React components; Migrated user records to a sharded Postgres cluster.\"\n\nBad: \"Security; Authentication; Login Page.\"\nGood: \"Identity management; Data encryption at rest.\"\n\n## Tasks, stories, bugs, and refactors\n\n- Every task needs a concrete \"done when\" state.\n- Avoid blank tasks such as \"Investigate latency\" unless the output is named.\n- Use active voice.\n\nBad: \"Perform an investigation into the latency situation.\"\nGood: \"Done when: the report identifies the three highest-latency queries in the /orders endpoint.\"\n\nBad: \"As a user, I want the utilization of an automated system.\"\nGood: \"As a user, I want to reset my password through an email link so I can regain access in under 2 minutes.\"\n\n## Vocabulary\n\n- Use exact technical terms when they are accurate. Replace metaphors only when they hide the cause.\n- Prefer \"essential\" or \"required\" over vague intensifiers such as \"vital.\"\n- Replace \"bottleneck\" with the specific shortage, delay, queue, lock, or capacity limit when known.\n- Prefer \"plan\" or \"spec\" over \"blueprint\" unless discussing an actual final design.\n- Prefer \"limit\" or \"maximum\" over \"ceiling.\"\n- Prefer \"use\" over \"utilization.\"\n- Prefer \"implement\" over \"implementation\" when describing action.\n- Prefer \"communicate with,\" \"connect to,\" or \"call\" over \"interface with.\"\n- Prefer \"affect\" over \"impact\" as a verb.\n- Delete \"basically,\" \"actually,\" \"quite,\" \"it should be noted that,\" and similar throat-clearing.\n- Replace \"at this point in time\" with \"now.\"\n\n## Agent review checklist\n\nBefore returning an artefact, check:\n\n- Does it move from problem to solution in a clear order?\n- Do subtasks explain how to achieve the milestone or why the milestone matters?\n- Did pruning preserve assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria?\n- Is every task's done state visible and verifiable?\n- Do important terms or conclusions appear where readers will notice them?\n- Does it sound like a capable colleague wrote it?\n"
}
```
