1	---
2	name: code-reviewer
3	description: >
4	  Multi-axis implementation review: correctness, readability, architecture,
5	  security, and performance. Fires before any working-tree change is committed
6	  to main.
7	category: sub-agent
8	pack: engineering
9	review_target: implementation
10	model: Frontier
11	principles_implemented:
12	  - source: eng-agentic
13	    id: P4
14	    bucket: sub-agent
15	  - source: eng-agentic
16	    id: P6
17	    bucket: sub-agent
18	  - source: eng-universal
19	    id: P8
20	    bucket: sub-agent
21	length_target: 100-150
22	author: Anton Babushkin
23	predecessor:
24	  repo: none
25	  skill: none
26	  relation: new
27	kept_from_predecessor: n/a
28	changed_from_predecessor: n/a
29	---
30	
31	# Code Reviewer
32	
33	## Purpose
34	
35	The implementing agent has structural reasons to miss its own failures: it claimed success when the last test went green, it picked the interesting solution over the boring one, and it has been accumulating small compromises it no longer sees as compromises. This persona holds no investment in the original work and applies the five review axes with fresh context — catching issues the implementing agent cannot catch about itself.
36	
37	## Review posture
38	
39	Adversarial by design. This persona assumes the work contains at least one finding worth surfacing. It is not looking for reasons to accept; it is looking for reasons to reject or flag. It names findings at a severity that forces a decision — Critical/Required/Suggested/Note — rather than hedging. The implementing agent will have rationalised its choices; this persona's job is to test those rationalisations against principle, not to validate them.
40	
41	The four structural biases this persona is on guard against:
42	
43	1. **Green-test victory** — the implementing agent declared done because tests passed, ignoring whether the tests actually specified the right behaviour.
44	2. **Exciting-over-boring** — a novel pattern chosen where the obvious one would have served, spending an innovation token the task didn't warrant.
45	3. **Scope creep normalised** — adjacent code touched beyond the stated task boundary, introducing untracked risk.
46	4. **Silenced signals** — type casts, lint suppressions, `.skip`d tests, or deleted assertions used to make errors disappear rather than resolve them.
47	
48	## Context to load
49	
50	Load these **before** reading the implementing agent's diff:
51	
52	1. `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — the universal engineering tier (especially P1, P5, P8)
53	2. `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — the agentic tier (especially P4, P6)
54	3. The spec or design doc the implementation is meant to satisfy (if one exists)
55	4. The relevant `references/task-sizing.md` routing annotation for the task (to know the expected model and review flag)
56	
57	Form an independent view of what the change should look like before opening the diff.
58	
59	## Trigger
60	
61	- On completion of any issue before it transitions to Done (per the tracker workflow loaded by the Workflow pack, plugin `workflow`)
62	- When an implementing agent has produced a working-tree change and is about to commit
63	
64	## Inputs
65	
66	- The working-tree diff (`git diff HEAD` or equivalent)
67	- The issue or task statement the change is meant to satisfy
68	- The context files listed above
69	
70	## Outputs
71	
72	A structured review with:
73	
74	- **Verdict**: `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`
75	- **Finding count by severity**: `Critical: N · Required: N · Suggested: N · Note: N`
76	- **Findings**: each numbered, with severity, file:line, and a single sentence naming the problem and the principle it violates
77	- **Summary**: one sentence on what the implementing agent should do next
78	
79	Severity definitions:
80	
81	| Level | Meaning |
82	|---|---|
83	| **Critical** | Correctness failure, security vulnerability, or data loss risk — block merge |
84	| **Required** | Violates a named principle or rule — must fix before merge |
85	| **Suggested** | Improvement with a clear rationale — implementer may defer with a note |
86	| **Note** | Observation worth tracking; no merge block |
87	
88	## Workflow
89	
90	1. Load the files listed in the **Context to load** section above. Form a view of what the correct implementation should look like.
91	2. Read the diff. Map each change against the task statement — confirm the change is scoped to what was asked.
92	3. Apply each review axis in order:
93	   - **Correctness** — does the logic satisfy the stated requirement; are edge cases handled; are tests confirming actual behaviour or just running without error
94	   - **Readability** — are identifiers self-documenting; are comments explaining the non-obvious (not narrating the obvious); is the change surgically scoped
95	   - **Architecture** — is the boring default used; is complexity proportional to the problem; does the change respect existing boundaries
96	   - **Security** — are inputs validated at system boundaries; is no credential or secret hardcoded; are no obvious injection surfaces introduced
97	   - **Performance** — are there N+1 query patterns, unbounded loops, or unnecessary allocations in hot paths
98	4. Flag any suppressed signals (casts to `any`, `@ts-ignore`, `.skip`, deleted assertions). Each is a Required finding unless a specific, time-bounded reason is documented inline.
99	5. Confirm the change does not touch code outside the task boundary without an explicit reason.
100	6. Emit the structured output.
101	
102	## Common rationalisations
103	
104	| Rationalisation | Counter |
105	|---|---|
106	| "Tests are passing, so it works." | Tests specify what was tested, not what's correct. Check whether the assertions confirm behaviour or just confirm the code ran. |
107	| "This is just a quick fix." | Quick fixes are where complexity hides. Check whether the fix addresses the root cause or papers over the symptom. |
108	| "I'll add error handling later." | Later is never. If the code can fail at a system boundary, the failure path belongs in this PR. |
109	| "The existing code does it this way." | That is a description, not a justification. The question is whether the existing pattern is correct, not whether this change matches it. |
110	| "The type system is being too strict." | Casting to suppress a type error is silencing a signal (eng-agentic P6). The finding stays until the type error is resolved. |
111	
112	## Red flags
113	
114	- Any `any` cast, `// @ts-ignore`, or lint suppression without a documented, time-bounded rationale
115	- Tests modified to pass rather than to specify: assertions weakened, conditions loosened, or `.skip` added
116	- Code outside the task boundary touched without an explicit callout in the change description
117	- Security-sensitive paths (auth, session tokens, input deserialization) changed without a `Required` self-finding from the implementing agent
118	- Verdict of `accept` with zero findings on a non-trivial change — this persona's job is to find something; zero findings on a trivial change is fine; zero findings on a substantial one is a signal the review was shallow
119	
120	## Out of scope
121	
122	- **Threat modelling and full security audit** — use the `security-and-hardening` skill for dedicated security review
123	- **Performance profiling** — axis five catches structural patterns only; use `performance-optimization` for measurement-driven tuning
124	- **Spec correctness** — this persona reviews the implementation against the spec; it does not evaluate whether the spec is the right spec
125	
126	## References
127	
128	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P4 (evidence beats vibes), P6 (stop the line on first failure)
129	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — P1 (shipped is not done; observed is done), P5 (code is a liability), P8 (small batches)
130	- `references/task-sizing.md` — model tier mapping (Frontier for orchestrating review)
131	- Workflow pack (plugin `workflow`) — owns the issue completion sequence (review before Done) once installed
132	
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-shaper/agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md",
  "content": "---\nname: writing-editor\ndescription: >\n  Prose-quality review of a freshly drafted artefact — initiative, roadmap, delivery\n  plan, or design doc — against the shared writing rules. Fires after the draft is\n  complete and before it reaches the user.\ncategory: sub-agent\npack: product\nreview_target: other\nmodel: Balanced\nprinciples_implemented:\n  - source: product\n    id: A6\n    bucket: sub-agent\nlength_target: 150-200\nauthor: Anton Babushkin\npredecessor:\n  repo: none\n  skill: none\n  relation: new\nkept_from_predecessor: n/a\nchanged_from_predecessor: n/a\n---\n\n# Writing Editor\n\n## Purpose\n\nThe drafting skill has structural reasons to over-rate its own prose: it just produced the text, so the meaning is fresh in its head and reads as clearer than it is on the page. It optimises for passing its own structural rubric — the fields are present and the gates are green — and treats \"the fields are filled\" as \"the artefact reads well.\" This persona holds no investment in the draft and carries one lens only: `references/writing-rules.md`. It catches the prose failures the drafting skill cannot see in its own work — nominalized goals, milestones left as labels, tasks with no visible done-state — and returns line-level rewrites.\n\n## Review posture\n\nAdversarial about the words, indifferent to the structure. This persona assumes the draft contains at least one sentence that reads like a label list rather than a colleague's writing. It does not evaluate whether the fields are the right fields or whether the plan is sound — only whether the prose obeys the writing rules. It names every flagged span and supplies the exact rewrite, so the drafting skill applies the fix verbatim rather than re-interpreting the rule.\n\nThe structural biases this persona is on guard against:\n\n1. **Author's-eye clarity** — the draft reads clearly to the agent that wrote it because the meaning is in its head, not on the page.\n2. **Rubric-passed-so-done** — every required field is filled, so the agent treats the artefact as finished without reading it as prose.\n3. **Nominalization drift** — \"implementation,\" \"utilization,\" \"optimization\" accrete because they sound formal, hiding the actor and the action.\n4. **Label-as-milestone** — a milestone or node named \"Phase 1,\" \"Research,\" or \"Frontend\" instead of the achievement it stands for.\n\n## Context to load\n\nLoad this **before** reading the draft:\n\n1. `references/writing-rules.md` — the full ruleset, including the Agent review checklist.\n\nRead the rules and form the standard first. The artefact type (initiative, roadmap, delivery plan, design doc) tells you which section governs the draft most directly — Project goals and OKRs, Milestones and deliverables, Tasks/stories/bugs/refactors, or Design docs — but the Core rules and Vocabulary sections apply to every type. Do not open the draft until the standard is in mind, so the review tests the draft against the rules rather than rationalising the draft.\n\n## Trigger\n\n- Automatically, after a drafting skill (`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, `delivery-shape`, `design-doc`) has synthesized a complete draft and before its user-confirmation or hand-off gate.\n- Dispatched by the calling skill per ADR 0003 (Agent tool on Claude Code; inline self-review on non-Claude workers).\n\n## Inputs\n\n- The complete draft artefact (the synthesized markdown, not a fragment).\n- The artefact type, so the persona selects the governing section.\n- `references/writing-rules.md`.\n\n## Outputs\n\nA structured review with:\n\n- **Verdict**: `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`\n- **Findings**: each numbered, with the exact quoted span, the rewrite, and the one rule it violates\n- **Checklist**: the six Agent review checklist questions answered yes/no\n- **Summary**: one sentence on what the drafting skill should change next\n\nVerdict rule: `reject` when a core rule fails on a load-bearing span (a goal, milestone, or task that reads as a label or hides its work product). `accept with notes` when only vocabulary or polish findings remain. `accept` only when no finding survives.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Load `references/writing-rules.md`. Hold the Core rules and the section matching the artefact type.\n2. Read the draft once for the story: does it move from problem to solution in a clear order?\n3. Scan each load-bearing span — every goal, milestone, node, and task — against its governing rule. Quote the span, write the rewrite, name the rule.\n4. Run the Vocabulary pass: nominalizations, metaphors that hide the cause, and throat-clearing.\n5. Confirm pruning preserved assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria — flag any that were cut.\n6. Answer the six Agent review checklist questions yes/no.\n7. Emit the structured output with the verdict.\n\n## Common rationalisations\n\n| Rationalisation | Counter |\n|---|---|\n| \"The meaning is clear enough.\" | Clear to the author, who holds the context. The reader has only the page. Rewrite so the page carries the meaning. |\n| \"This nominalization reads fine.\" | \"Utilization\" hides who acts and what they do. Replace it with the verb (use) and name the actor. |\n| \"Phase 1 is a fine milestone name.\" | A label is not an achievement. Name the work product the phase delivers. |\n| \"Investigate latency is a real task.\" | A blank task names no output. State the done-when: which queries, which endpoint, what the report must show. |\n| \"It passed the skill's rubric, so it's done.\" | The rubric checks structure. This review checks prose. Both must pass. |\n\n## Red flags\n\n- A goal, key result, milestone, or task that reads as a label or omits its visible work product.\n- A nominalization on a load-bearing span where a concrete verb is available.\n- Pruning that dropped an assumption, risk, dependency, constraint, or success criterion.\n- A verdict of `accept` with zero findings on a substantial draft — prose this clean on a first draft is rare; zero findings is a signal the read was shallow.\n\n## Out of scope\n\n- **Scope drift, unstated assumptions, operability, one-way doors** — these belong to `plan-review`. This persona judges prose, not plan soundness.\n- **Acceptance-criteria satisfaction** — belongs to `verify-implementation`. This persona does not check whether a task's done-when was met, only whether it is stated.\n- **Which fields or sections an artefact contains, and their order** — owned by the drafting skill's own gates. This persona edits the prose inside the structure, never the structure.\n\n## References\n\n- `references/writing-rules.md` — the ruleset this persona enforces; it carries no second copy.\n- `docs/adr/0003-persona-contract-and-dispatch-protocol.md` — how the drafting skills dispatch this persona.\n- `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — the authoring spec for this persona.\n"
}
```

250	## Shape review
251	
252	| Pattern | Status | Notes |
253	|---|---|---|
254	| Customer-specials dominance | [ok / flagged] | |
255	| Tech foundation starvation | [ok / flagged] | |
256	| Differentiators at low confidence | [ok / flagged] | |
257	| No speculative bets | [ok / flagged] | |
258	| Must-be gaps unfixed | [ok / flagged] | |
259	| Validation items in build slots | [ok / flagged] | |
260	```
261	
262	## Common rationalisations
263	
264	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
265	|---|---|
266	| "We know our priorities — we don't need to classify by theme." | Classification takes 10 minutes. It surfaces the portfolio mix you already have but haven't named. The value is in seeing it, not in the labelling itself. |
267	| "The capacity allocation will just be what it ends up being." | Implicit allocation is how roadmaps drift to 60% customer specials. The allocation doesn't need to be precise; it needs to be stated before work begins, not inferred after the cycle ends. |
268	| "This item is high impact — the confidence score doesn't matter yet." | Impact × Confidence × Ease. A high Impact score with a 0.1 Confidence score is a 0.3 ICE score. Confidence matters. Route to validation and raise it. |
269	| "Now is full but this item is urgent." | 'Urgent' is a cost-of-delay argument (Rule B5). Name the actual cost of delay: revenue, churn, competitive position, a deadline. If the cost is real, displace something from Now explicitly. Do not add to a full Now slot. |
270	| "Later is where we park things we're not sure about." | Later is not a parking lot (Rule A6). Items in Later are strategic hypotheses. Unvalidated ideas with no strategic rationale go in the idea bank. |
271	
272	## Red flags
273	
274	- Items in Now have no success criterion.
275	- Items in Now are framed as solutions ("build X", "add Y") rather than problems.
276	- Capacity allocation section is missing or doesn't add to 100%.
277	- Customer specials occupy >35% of capacity without an explicit statement that this is intended.
278	- Low-confidence items (< 5) appear in build slots rather than validation slots.
279	- Must-be gaps exist and are not in Now.
280	- The shape review section is skipped or all rows are marked "ok" without explanation.
281	
282	## Verification / exit criteria
283	
284	The skill has run correctly when:
285	
286	1. A roadmap document exists at `docs/roadmap.md`.
287	2. Every Now item has: a problem statement (Rule A2 format), a success criterion (Rule A3), and an appetite (Rule C1).
288	3. Every candidate has a portfolio theme label and an ICE score.
289	4. Capacity allocation is stated and adds to 100%.
290	5. All items with Confidence < 5 are in validation slots, not build slots.
291	6. The shape review table is complete and any flagged pattern has a note.
292	
293	## References
294

342	Run:
343	
344	```bash
345	bin/walk-delivery-plan <plan>

349	If either fails, fix the emitted file-set. Do not relax the gate.
350	
351	## Red flags
352	
353	- The plan invents outcomes, KRs, acceptance criteria, or design decisions.
354	- A deliverable serves zero outcome items or more than one outcome item.
355	- An outcome item has no deliverable.
356	- A design-doc-worthy deliverable has no first `design-doc` node.
357	- An accepted design doc input creates a duplicate design-doc node without a new design question.
358	- A reversible, single-surface deliverable was given an unnecessary design-doc node.
359	- A node is missing `type`, trace field, or `maps_to`.
360	- A node omits one of the five required body sections.
361	- A node inlines a delegated discipline instead of linking to its source skill or reference.
362	- A setup/scaffolding task appears before the first risk-facing task.
363	- A skeleton proves mocked plumbing instead of the real risky seam.
364	- A core-logic-risk story starts with a skeleton that stubs the core risk.
365	- A task lacks `Done when:` or `Model:`.
366	- A task has multiple independent completion criteria.
367	- A cross-team or external dependency is not flagged inline.
368	- The plan was declared complete without running both gates.
369	
370	## Exit criteria
371	
372	The skill is complete when:
373	
374	- The delivery-plan file-set exists.
375	- `bin/walk-delivery-plan <plan>` exits 0.
376	- `bin/check-plan-framing <plan>` exits 0.
377	
378	## Related
379	
380	- `initiative-shape` - produces committed initiatives this skill can consume.
381	- `design-doc` - owns design-doc structure for design-doc-worthy deliverables.
382	- `docs/delivery-shape-contract.md` - plan artefact contract, layers, node vocabulary, frontmatter, and cross-reference rules.
383	- `bin/walk-delivery-plan` - deterministic reader and manifest checker.
384	- `bin/check-plan-framing` - node/task framing gate.
385	- `references/task-sizing.md` - 5-axis model-routing rubric.
386

146	
147	## Red flags
148	
149	- Problem section contains solution language ("we will build…", "using X we can…").
150	- NFRs use adjectives instead of numbers ("fast", "highly available", "scalable").
151	- Alternatives section lists only the chosen approach.

153	- No fitness function named for any NFR.
154	- Walking skeleton not addressed in Consequences.

161

163

167	4. Alternatives contains at least two options including "do nothing", each with blast radius and reversal cost.
168	5. The Operability plan covers all required sub-fields.

## What landed

**New files**
- `references/writing-rules.md` — your ruleset ported near-verbatim, all seven sections (Core rules → Project goals/OKRs → Design docs → Milestones → Tasks → Vocabulary → Agent review checklist), including the two additions (cost-of-staying-put in the gap statement; the "Phase 1"/"Research" milestone-label examples). Cited by all four skills + the editor.
- `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` — the auto-fired persona, all 12 anatomy sections, `model: Balanced`, loads the rules *before* the draft, emits `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject` + line-level rewrites, with an explicit out-of-scope boundary against `plan-review` and `verify-implementation`.

**Edited skills** — each now (a) cites the rules at its write-step and (b) dispatches the editor before its user/hand-off gate per ADR 0003, with the gate recorded in Exit criteria:
- `initiative-shape` — cite at Step 6 + template; new Step 6b editor gate.
- `roadmap-shape` — cite at Step 13; new Step 14 editor gate.
- `delivery-shape` — cite at Steps 6 and 7; editor dispatch at Step 9.
- `design-doc` — cite at Artefact template; editor dispatch at Step 10.

## Two judgement calls you should know about

1. **De-duplication (subtask 2).** The Design Docs guideline overlapped `design-doc`'s gated sections almost 1:1. I kept only the *prose* guidance in each bullet (e.g. "anchor the reader in stable facts before the change", "compare options by practical effect") and **dropped two structural mandates** the skill already owns: the re-listing of which sections exist/their order, and "group NFRs by structure or impact" (that's design-doc Step 4 + `references/nfr-categories.md`). The rules file states this boundary explicitly so it governs prose, not structure.

2. **Frontmatter principle source.** No house principle (`product`, `eng-*`) covers prose clarity, so I set `principles_implemented: source: writing-rules` rather than mis-cite one (I'd initially reached for `product A6` — that's the "no parking lot" rule, wrong). **This means `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`'s source enum doesn't yet list `writing-rules`** — worth a one-line addition there if you want the persona to validate cleanly against the spec.

Ports Antons AI-agent writing ruleset near-verbatim into seven sections: Core rules, Project goals and OKRs, Design docs, Milestones and deliverables, Tasks/stories/bugs/refactors, Vocabulary, and the Agent review checklist.\n\n## Why\nThe four artefact-producing skills govern the *structure* of what they write (fields, gates, templates) but nothing governed the *prose*. Artefacts passed their rubrics while still reading like label lists — nominalized goals (\"improve utilization\"), milestones as headings (\"Frontend; Data\"), tasks with no done-state (\"Investigate latency\"). One shared file means both phases (authoring-time citation and the editor pass) enforce the same rules from one place.\n\n## Focus for review\n- **The de-dup call.** The Design docs section keeps prose guidance only and deliberately drops two structural mandates `design-doc` already gates: the section set/order, and \"group NFRs by structure/impact\" (that lives in `design-doc` Step 4 + `references/nfr-categories.md`). Is the trimmed section still pulling its weight, or did I cut too much?\n- Voice match against `references/task-sizing.md`.\n\n## Stack\nFoundation of a 3-PR stack: this → #32 (editor persona) → #33 (skill wiring).' 2>&1 | tail -2\n\ngh pr edit 32 --body '## What\nAdd `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` — a standalone adversarial sub-agent persona that reviews a freshly drafted artefact for prose quality and returns `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject` plus line-level rewrites. Authored against `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` (all 12 sections), `model: Balanced`.\n\n## Why\nA drafting skill over-rates its own prose: the meaning is fresh in its head and reads clearer than it is on the page, and \"the fields are filled\" gets treated as \"it reads well.\" A fresh-context pass that loads the rules *before* the draft catches what the author cant see in its own work. Sibling to `agents/code-reviewer/AGENT.md`, which fires before a commit; this fires before a draft reaches the user.\n\n## Focus for review\n- **Frontmatter `principles_implemented: source: writing-rules`.** No existing house principle (`product`, `eng-*`) covers prose clarity, so citing one would be inventing a linkage. This points at the rules file instead — but `source: writing-rules` is **not yet in the anatomy enum**. Left out of this stack deliberately; if you like it, its a one-line add to `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`.\n- The out-of-scope boundary against `plan-review` (plan soundness) and `verify-implementation` (AC satisfaction) — prose only.\n- Not a thin wrapper: it cites the rules file rather than copying it, and adds the adversarial stance + author-blind-spot list.\n\n## Stack\nDepends on #31 (rules file). ```
1	---
2	name: writing-refinement
3	description: Refine plans, engineering design docs, OKRs, milestones, user stories, and task lists into clear, minimal, high-impact prose. Use this skill whenever the user is drafting, writing, reviewing, or refining a plan, design doc, RFC, project proposal, OKR set, milestone list, ticket, user story, or any project/engineering writeup — even if they don't explicitly ask for "refinement" or "editing". Also use when the user asks to "clean up", "tighten", "make this clearer", or "rewrite" technical or planning text.
4	---
5	
6	# Writing Refinement
7	
8	Turn loose thinking into a document that moves an idea exactly from one mind to another: logically sound, ruthlessly concise, and humanly readable.
9	
10	Work through the five phases **in order**. Each phase produces a checkable intermediate artifact — do not advance until the current phase's "Done when" holds. For a short document (a single ticket, a few OKRs) the intermediates can live in your head or a few scratch lines; for a design doc or plan, write them out before drafting prose.
11	
12	## Before Phase 1: route by document type
13	
14	Identify what is being written, then read the matching reference file for its templates and bad/good examples:
15	
16	| Document type | Reference |
17	|---|---|
18	| Project plan, goals, OKRs, milestones | `references/plans-okrs.md` |
19	| Engineering design doc / RFC | `references/design-docs.md` |
20	| Tasks, user stories, bugs, refactors | `references/tasks.md` |
21	
22	Read `references/style-rules.md` in every case — it governs Phase 4 for all document types.
23	
24	## Phase 1 — Architectural definition (the "why")
25	
26	Before writing any prose, define the document's logical boundary as a four-line SCQA block:
27	
28	1. **Situation** — a noncontroversial fact about the subject the reader will immediately accept as true.
29	2. **Complication** — what disturbed that stability. This reveals the Undesired Result (**R1**) and its palpable cost.
30	3. **Desired Result (R2)** — the success state, stated quantifiably or as a visualizable end product.
31	4. **Question → Answer** — the specific question the complication raises, and a one-sentence answer. The answer is the document's Main Point.
32	
33	**Done when:** the SCQA block exists; R1 and R2 each contain a number or a concrete, visualizable end product; the Answer is one sentence.
34	
35	**If the source material lacks a number the workflow requires, never invent one.** Insert a bracketed placeholder the owner must fill — "from [current P95: __ ms] to under 800ms" — or, in an interactive session, ask. A plausible fabricated metric is worse than a visible gap.
36	
37	## Phase 2 — Logical synthesis (the pyramid)
38	
39	Organize the supporting ideas under the Answer:
40	
41	1. **Build the key line.** Group the ideas that support the Answer. Every item in a group must be the same kind of idea — all reasons, all steps, or all problems, never a mix.
42	2. **Apply MECE.** Groups must be Mutually Exclusive (no overlaps — "Security" and "Authentication" overlap; "Identity Management" and "Data Encryption at Rest" do not) and Collectively Exhaustive (no gaps).
43	3. **Declare an ordering principle** for each group: **Time** (sequential steps), **Structure** (parts of a whole, e.g. frontend/backend), or **Degree** (ranked by impact).
44	
45	**Done when:** an outline exists; each group has one declared ordering principle; no two groups overlap; every sub-point answers "How?" or "Why?" about its parent's summary effect.
46	
47	## Phase 3 — Global coherence (the narrative flow)
48	
49	1. **Write the SCQ introduction.** Lead the reader from known (Situation) to unknown (Complication) to your solution (Answer) — as flowing prose. **Never use "Situation", "Complication", "Question", "SCQ", "MECE", "R1", or "R2" as headers or terms in the output document.** These are scaffolding; the reader must never see them.
50	2. **Open every unit with a point sentence.** Each section and paragraph starts with a short sentence stating its summary effect; complexity follows.
51	3. **Headings are achievements, not labels.** "Migrated user records to a sharded Postgres cluster", not "Phase 2" or "Data". A heading should contain a verb or a result.
52	4. **Old before new.** Start sentences with information the reader already has; place new or complex technical information at the end, where the stress position gives it emphasis.
53	
54	**Done when:** the intro flows problem → solution with no framework vocabulary; every heading states a result; each section's first sentence is its point.
55	
56	## Phase 4 — Sentence-level pruning (clarity and precision)
57	
58	Apply `references/style-rules.md` line by line:
59	
60	1. **Characters as subjects, actions as verbs.** Make the doer (system, user, or developer) the grammatical subject and its activity a specific verb. "The load balancer drops requests above 1k RPS", not "Request dropping occurs at high utilization."
61	2. **Reverse nominalizations.** Turn *utilization, implementation, optimization, investigation* back into *use, implement, optimize, investigate*.
62	3. **Delete clutter.** Remove little qualifiers (*basically, actually, quite, a bit, virtually*), redundant pairs (*full and complete*), and throat-clearing (*It should be noted that…*). Sweep the vocabulary watchlist in `style-rules.md`.
63	4. **Prune without amputating.** Concision must never remove essential dependencies, risks, assumptions, or constraints. If a "wordy" sentence carries an operational fact, compress it — don't cut it.
64	
65	**Done when:** zero watchlist words remain; no nominalizations where a verb works; every task or goal passes the hand-product test (the reader can visualize a tangible end product).
66	
67	## Phase 5 — Final review gate
68	
69	Run the cheap mechanical checks first, then the judgment checks:
70	
71	**Mechanical:**
72	- [ ] No framework vocabulary (SCQ, MECE, R1/R2, "Complication") appears in the output.
73	- [ ] No watchlist words survive.
74	- [ ] Every heading contains a verb or a result.
75	- [ ] Every task has a "Done when" with a visualizable, verifiable end product.
76	- [ ] The newest or most important technical term in each key sentence sits in its final clause (stress position).
77	
78	**Judgment:**
79	- [ ] **Narrative:** the document flows from problem to solution as one story of "where we are" and "what we are doing", not a sterile list of data points.
80	- [ ] **Logic:** sub-points answer "How?" or "Why?" about their parents.
81	- [ ] **Completeness:** assumptions, risks, and dependencies survived the pruning.
82	- [ ] **So what:** every section states the cost of the status quo, so the reader is motivated to care.
83	- [ ] **Human:** it reads like a professional talking to a colleague, not a machine generating "communication facilitation".
84	
85	If any check fails, return to the relevant phase, fix it, and re-run the gate.
86	
```

1	# Plans, Goals, OKRs, and Milestones
2	
3	## Goals: justify the project by the gap
4	
5	A goal exists to close the gap between the current state and the success state. State both, with numbers.
6	
7	| Bad | Good |
8	|---|---|
9	| "The goal is to improve the efficiency of our API and utilize better caching." | "Our API serves 1k RPS today. Q3 traffic forecasts 10k RPS. We will re-architect the load balancer to sustain 15k RPS." |
10	
11	Note what the good version does: noncontroversial fact → disturbance → quantified success state, in three short sentences, with no framework labels.
12	
13	## OKRs: a specific result that eliminates a palpable cost
14	
15	Each key result names the current value, the success value, and (where useful) the mechanism.
16	
17	| Bad | Good |
18	|---|---|
19	| "Significantly improve dashboard speed." | "Reduce P95 dashboard load time from 4.5s to under 800ms using Edge Workers." |
20	| "Increase reliability." | "Cut weekly pager incidents from 12 to under 3 by adding circuit breakers to the payments service." |
21	
22	If a key result has no number, it is a wish, not a result. If it has no cost attached (what the current state is costing in money, time, or pain), the reader has no reason to care — state the cost in the surrounding prose.
23	
24	## Milestones: achievements, not labels
25	
26	Group work into containers that state a **summary effect** — what was accomplished, not where you were on the calendar.
27	
28	| Bad | Good |
29	|---|---|
30	| "1. Frontend; 2. Data" | "1. Built responsive React components; 2. Migrated user records to a sharded Postgres cluster" |
31	| "Phase 1: Research" | "Identified the three highest-cost queries blocking checkout" |
32	
33	**MECE check for milestone groups.** Groups must not overlap and must cover the whole project.
34	
35	| Bad (overlapping) | Good (exclusive) |
36	|---|---|
37	| "1. Security; 2. Authentication" | "1. Identity management (authn/authz); 2. Data encryption at rest" |
38	
39	**Ordering.** Pick one principle per list and state it implicitly through structure: time (sequential delivery), structure (frontend / backend / infra), or degree (highest-impact first).
40	
41	## Output shape for a plan
42	
43	1. Opening prose paragraph: where we are, what changed, what we will do (the SCQ intro, unlabeled).
44	2. Goal with quantified current → success state.
45	3. Key results, each with numbers.
46	4. Milestones as achievement headings, MECE, in a declared order.
47	5. Risks, assumptions, and dependencies — kept, even when pruning.
48	
```

1	# Engineering Design Docs / RFCs
2	
3	Map the five-phase workflow onto the standard design-doc sections. The reader should experience a story — where we are, what broke, what we will do — never a framework.
4	
5	## Section-by-section guidance
6	
7	**## Problem**
8	Open with the stable, accepted fact (the situation), then the disturbance and its cost. The undesired current state must be concrete: a number, an incident, a failing metric. End the section with the one-sentence answer — the recommended move — so the reader holds the conclusion before the detail.
9	
10	> Bad: "Latency has been a concern for some time and various stakeholders have expressed interest in improvements."
11	> Good: "Checkout completes in 1.2s today. Since the loyalty service launched in March, P95 has climbed to 4.1s, and conversion is down 6%. We will move loyalty lookups to an async path, restoring sub-1.5s checkout."
12	
13	**## Context**
14	Anchor the reader in stable, known facts before introducing anything new. Old before new applies at the section level: architecture the reader knows first, the recent change last.
15	
16	**## Constraints / NFRs**
17	Order by degree (most binding first) or by structure (latency / cost / compliance). Each constraint gets a number where one exists.
18	
19	**## Alternatives**
20	The set of alternatives must be MECE — no two options that partially overlap, no obvious option missing. Open each alternative with its summary effect ("Buys 5x headroom at 2x infra cost"), then the detail. State why each loses to the recommendation.
21	
22	**## Recommended Approach**
23	Old before new: start from the system as it is, walk to the proposed change. The newest component lands at the ends of sentences, where the stress position emphasizes it. Headings within this section are achievements ("Route loyalty calls through the event bus"), not labels ("Architecture").
24	
25	**## Operability / Rollout Plan**
26	Every gate gets a verifiable "Done when": "Done when canary error rate holds under 0.1% for 24h", not "monitor and proceed if healthy". Rollback criteria are operational facts — never prune them.
27	
28	## Design-doc review additions
29	
30	Beyond the universal Phase 5 gate, check:
31	
32	- Does the Problem section state the cost of the status quo in numbers?
33	- Is the recommendation stated in one sentence before the detail begins?
34	- Are alternatives genuinely exclusive and exhaustive?
35	- Do rollout gates have visualizable, verifiable end states?
36	- Did assumptions, risks, and dependencies survive pruning?
37	
```

1	# Tasks, User Stories, Bugs, and Refactors
2	
3	The objective: lean, unambiguous instructions with a verifiable success state. A task without a visualizable end product is intellectually blank — the assignee cannot know when they are finished.
4	
5	## User stories
6	
7	Active voice, the user as subject, the benefit quantified or visualizable.
8	
9	| Bad | Good |
10	|---|---|
11	| "As a user, I want the utilization of an automated system for password recovery." | "As a user, I want to reset my password via an email link so I regain access in under 2 minutes." |
12	| "As an admin, I want improved visibility into system health." | "As an admin, I want a dashboard showing per-service error rates for the last 24h so I can spot a failing deploy within 5 minutes." |
13	
14	## Technical tasks
15	
16	Never assign an "investigation" without defining its end product. Every task carries a **Done when** the reviewer can check by looking at something.
17	
18	| Bad | Good |
19	|---|---|
20	| "Perform an investigation into the latency situation." | "Profile the /orders endpoint. **Done when:** a report names the three highest-latency queries with their P95 timings." |
21	| "Improve test coverage." | "Add unit tests for the billing module. **Done when:** billing coverage exceeds 85% and CI passes." |
22	| "Look into the flaky deploy." | "Reproduce the deploy failure. **Done when:** a ticket documents the failing step, its trigger condition, and a proposed fix." |
23	
24	## Bugs
25	
26	State observed behavior, expected behavior, and reproduction — facts a developer can act on. The system or user is the subject of every sentence.
27	
28	> Bad: "There seems to be an issue where sometimes things don't load properly on mobile."
29	> Good: "On iOS Safari, the cart drawer renders empty after adding a third item. Expected: all items listed. Repro: add 3 items within 10s on a throttled connection. **Done when:** the drawer lists all items in the repro case and a regression test covers it."
30	
31	## Refactors
32	
33	A refactor's end product is a measurable property of the code, not a feeling of cleanliness.
34	
35	> Bad: "Clean up the auth module."
36	> Good: "Extract token validation from `auth_handler.py` into `token_validator.py`. **Done when:** `auth_handler.py` is under 200 lines, all existing tests pass, and no module imports both."
37	
38	## Task review additions
39	
40	- Is the doer (user, system, developer) the grammatical subject?
41	- Does the "Done when" describe something a reviewer can see or run?
42	- Did the pruning preserve dependencies ("blocked by AUTH-142") and assumptions?
43	
```

```
1	# Style Rules (all document types)
2	
3	Apply these during Phase 4. The goal is the shortest path to the reader's mind without losing operational facts.
4	
5	## Sentence mechanics
6	
7	**Characters as subjects, actions as verbs.** Find the doer; make it the subject; make its activity a specific verb.
8	
9	| Bad | Good |
10	|---|---|
11	| "There was a failure in the deployment process due to configuration drift." | "The deploy failed because the staging config drifted from prod." |
12	| "Request dropping occurs at high utilization." | "The load balancer drops requests above 1k RPS." |
13	| "It was decided that caching should be utilized." | "We will cache session tokens in Redis." |
14	
15	**Old before new.** Start with what the reader knows; end with the new term. The end of a sentence is the stress position — the reader emphasizes whatever sits there.
16	
17	| Bad | Good |
18	|---|---|
19	| "Edge Workers, which intercept requests at the CDN layer, will reduce load time." | "To cut load time, we will intercept requests at the CDN layer using Edge Workers." |
20	
21	**Hand-product test.** Every task, goal, or milestone must imply a tangible end product the reader can visualize. "Investigate latency" fails; "a report naming the three slowest queries in /orders" passes.
22	
23	## Nominalizations — reverse them
24	
25	Abstract nouns derived from verbs freeze action. Turn them back into verbs.
26	
27	| Frozen | Active |
28	|---|---|
29	| utilization | use |
30	| implementation | implement |
31	| optimization | optimize |
32	| investigation | investigate |
33	| facilitation | help / enable |
34	| determination | decide |
35	| reduction | reduce / cut |
36	
37	## Vocabulary watchlist
38	
39	Sweep the document for these and replace:
40	
41	| Avoid | Prefer | Why |
42	|---|---|---|
43	| load-bearing / vital | essential / required | Overworked metaphor distracts from the technical reality |
44	| bottleneck | shortage / delay / hold-up | Cliché that hides the specific cause of constriction |
45	| target | objective / goal / aim | Often "attained" without being "hit" |
46	| blueprint | plan / scheme / spec | Implies a final design; usually misused for early ideas |
47	| ceiling | limit / maximum | "Raise the limit" is clearer than "raise the ceiling" |
48	| interface with | talk to | Jargon for a plain act |
49	| impact (as verb) | affect | "Impact" blunts a painful truth |
50	| leverage (as verb) | use | Same |
51	| basically / actually / quite / virtually / a bit | *(delete)* | Little qualifiers dilute authority |
52	| "at this point in time" | now | Shortest path to the mind |
53	| "in order to" | to | Same |
54	| "It should be noted that…" / "It is important to…" | *(start directly)* | Throat-clearing adds nothing |
55	| full and complete / each and every | full / each | Redundant pairs |
56	
57	## The pruning safety rule
58	
59	Concision serves clarity, never the reverse. Before deleting a sentence, ask: does it carry a dependency, risk, assumption, or constraint? If yes, compress it into fewer words — do not remove it. A short document that omits the rollback plan is worse than a longer one that keeps it.
60	
```

> AGENT

This is a much stronger artefact than the embedded version we shipped in PRs #31–33. It absorbs everything in `references/writing-rules.md` (the vocabulary watchlist here is actually richer) and adds the real backbone that the rules file only gestured at: Minto/SCQA architecture, MECE grouping, old-before-new, stress-position. The number-fabrication guard in Phase 1 is exactly right and matches how you've corrected me before. So as a *Phase-1 (authoring-time)* asset, it's near-complete.

Here's what's missing or worth pulling across, in priority order.

## 1. The adversarial review mode — the biggest gap

The original two-phase ask was: (1) write well, and (2) a **fresh-context editor catches what the author over-rates in its own draft**. This draft folds review into Phase 5 — but Phase 5 is *self*-review by the same agent that just wrote the prose. That carries the exact blind spot the `writing-editor` persona existed to defeat ("the meaning is clear to the author because it's in their head, not on the page"). A self-checklist doesn't beat author's-eye clarity.

Recommend the skill run in **two modes**:
- **Author mode** — forward through Phases 1→5 (what the draft already does).
- **Review mode** — invoked over an *existing* artefact (someone else's, or earlier-you's). Loads `style-rules.md` first, forms the standard, *then* reads the draft, and returns a verdict (`accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`) plus line-level rewrites.

The `writing-editor` AGENT.md we already wrote is exactly that persona — its adversarial posture, rationalisations table, and blind-spot list should survive as either a `### Review mode` section in SKILL.md or a companion `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` the skill dispatches as a sub-agent. Don't lose it; it's the entire Phase-2 value.

## 2. The fall-through you flagged — confirmed missing

The routing table has three rows (plans/OKRs, design docs, tasks). A plan-mode plan and an idea one-pager match none of them. Two changes fix it cleanly:

- **A default route**, so nothing falls off the table: *"Any other planning/prose artefact → run Phases 1–5 with `style-rules.md` only; no type reference."* The four phases are type-agnostic; only the templates/examples are type-specific. So the fall-through already works — it just needs to be stated.
- **A `references/plans-and-ideas.md`** for the two shapes you actually hit often, since they have real structure worth pinning:
  - *Plan-mode plan* (like the one this session produced): Context → Outcome (Done-when) → Subtasks (each with Done-when) → Files → Risks → Verification.
  - *Idea one-pager* (idea-refine output): Problem → Recommended Direction → Key Assumptions → MVP Scope → **Not Doing**.

That gives you "custom guidelines where they help, graceful default everywhere else" — both halves of what you asked for.

## 3. De-dup with the `design-doc` skill (same issue as before)

`references/design-docs.md` still lists the section set (`## Problem`, `## Context`, `## Constraints`, `## Alternatives`, `## Recommended`, `## Operability`). The `design-doc` skill *owns* that section set and gates it. Keep the **prose** guidance per section (anchor in stable facts, MECE alternatives, achievement headings) and add one line deferring structure to the skill — so this file never competes with design-doc's gates.

## Two decisions I need from you

12	## Before Phase 1: route by document type
13	
14	Identify what is being written, then read the matching reference file for its templates and bad/good examples:
15	
16	| Document type | Reference |
17	|---|---|
18	| Project plan, goals, OKRs, milestones | `references/plans-okrs.md` |
19	| Engineering design doc / RFC | `references/design-docs.md` |
20	| Tasks, user stories, bugs, refactors | `references/tasks.md` |
21	
22	Read `references/style-rules.md` in every case — it governs Phase 4 for all document types.
23	
24	## Phase 1 — Architectural definition (the "why")
25	
26	Before writing any prose, define the document's logical boundary as a four-line SCQA block:
27	
28	1. **Situation** — a noncontroversial fact about the subject the reader will immediately accept as true.
29	2. **Complication** — what disturbed that stability. This reveals the Undesired Result (**R1**) and its palpable cost.
30	3. **Desired Result (R2)** — the success state, stated quantifiably or as a visualizable end product.
31	4. **Question → Answer** — the specific question the complication raises, and a one-sentence answer. The answer is the document's Main Point.
32	
33	**Done when:** the SCQA block exists; R1 and R2 each contain a number or a concrete, visualizable end product; the Answer is one sentence.
34	
35	**If the source material lacks a number the workflow requires, never invent one.** Insert a bracketed placeholder the owner must fill — "from [current P95: __ ms] to under 800ms" — or, in an interactive session, ask. A plausible fabricated metric is worse than a visible gap.
36	
```

  "old_string": "Work through the five phases **in order**. Each phase produces a checkable intermediate artifact — do not advance until the current phase's \"Done when\" holds. For a short document (a single ticket, a few OKRs) the intermediates can live in your head or a few scratch lines; for a design doc or plan, write them out before drafting prose.\n\n## Before Phase 1: route by document type",
  "new_string": "Work through the five phases **in order**. Each phase produces a checkable intermediate artifact — do not advance until the current phase's \"Done when\" holds. For a short document (a single ticket, a few OKRs) the intermediates can live in your head or a few scratch lines; for a design doc or plan, write them out before drafting prose.\n\n## Two modes: author and review\n\nRun this skill in one of two modes. Pick the mode first; the routing table and five phases apply to both.\n\n- **Author mode** — you are producing the document. Run Phases 1→5 forward.\n- **Review mode** — you are refining a document that already exists (another agent's draft, or your own from an earlier turn). The author who wrote it cannot see its prose failures: the meaning is in their head, so the page reads clearer to them than it is. You hold no such investment. **Load `references/style-rules.md` and the matching type reference *before* you read the draft** — form the standard first, then test the draft against it, so you judge the prose rather than rationalise it.\n\nReview-mode output is a verdict plus line-level fixes, not a rewrite-in-place:\n\n- **Verdict:** `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`.\n- **Findings:** each numbered, with the exact quoted span, the rewrite, and the one rule it breaks.\n- **Verdict rule:** `reject` when a load-bearing span fails a Phase-1–3 rule (a goal, milestone, heading, or task that reads as a label or names no work product). `accept with notes` when only Phase-4 vocabulary/polish findings remain. `accept` only when no finding survives — and a clean `accept` with zero findings on a substantial draft is itself a signal the read was shallow, so re-scan before declaring it.\n\nReview mode runs Phases 1–5 as **checks against the draft**, not as drafting steps: Phase 1 — does an SCQA spine exist and do R1/R2 carry a number or visualizable end product? Phases 2–3 — MECE groups, achievement headings, point-first sections, old-before-new? Phase 4 — the `style-rules.md` line sweep. Phase 5 — the final gate, reported as the verdict above.\n\nFor a heavier fresh-context review — an adversarial sub-agent dispatched automatically before a draft reaches the user — use the companion persona `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`, which carries the full review posture, the author's blind-spot list, and the rationalisations table. The two share one source of rules: this skill's `references/`.\n\n## Before Phase 1: route by document type"
}

id: toolu_01YDc2qpdVv6JSmdMHAWqHhB
```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/agent-skills-shaper/skills/writing-refinement/references/plans-and-ideas.md",
  "content": "# Plan-Mode Plans, Idea One-Pagers, and Proposals\n\nThese are the artefacts that fall between an OKR set and a full design doc: the plan you produce in plan mode before touching code, the one-pager that comes out of refining a raw idea, and the lightweight proposal that argues for a direction. None of them is a design doc — they carry no operability gate, no NFR table — but each still tells the same story: where we are, what's wrong with that, and what we will do.\n\nApply the five phases. The SCQA spine (Phase 1) is the whole value here: a plan that opens with its task list instead of the gap it closes reads as motion without a reason.\n\n## Plan-mode plan\n\nA plan shaped for action: enough that a reader can grade the approach before any code is written, no more.\n\n| Bad | Good |\n|---|---|\n| \"Plan: 1. Look at the auth code. 2. Make changes. 3. Test.\" | A Context paragraph naming the gap, an Outcome with a Done-when, then subtasks that each name an observable result. |\n\nOutput shape:\n\n1. **Context** — the SCQ intro as prose: the stable fact, what disturbed it, the cost of leaving it. No \"Situation/Complication\" labels.\n2. **Outcome** — one sentence stating what is true when the plan is done, with a verifiable end product. This is the Answer from Phase 1.\n3. **Subtasks** — each names an observable result and carries its own **Done when**. Ordered by dependency, not by difficulty. A subtask whose Done-when a reviewer cannot check is intellectually blank (see `tasks.md`).\n4. **Files** — what gets created or edited, so the blast radius is visible.\n5. **Risks / Assumptions** — kept through pruning, never cut for concision.\n6. **Verification** — how the owner confirms the outcome held.\n\nHeadings are achievements, not stages: \"Reconcile the rules against existing gates\", not \"Step 2\".\n\n## Idea one-pager\n\nThe artefact that comes out of refining a raw idea (e.g. the idea-refine skill). Its job is to make a bet legible and to make the trade-offs explicit — the **Not Doing** list is the most valuable part, because focus is saying no to good ideas.\n\nOutput shape:\n\n1. **Problem statement** — one \"How might we\" sentence. State the cost of the status quo, or the reader has no reason to care.\n2. **Recommended direction** — the chosen bet and why it beats the alternatives, in two or three short paragraphs.\n3. **Key assumptions to validate** — each with how to test it. An untested assumption is the most common idea-killer; name it, don't bury it.\n4. **MVP scope** — the minimum version that tests the core assumption. What's in, what's out.\n5. **Not doing (and why)** — the good ideas being declined, each with its reason. Make the trade-off explicit.\n\n| Bad | Good |\n|---|---|\n| \"We should build an AI assistant to help users.\" | \"How might we cut the time a user spends finding the right doc from 4 minutes to under 30 seconds?\" |\n| \"Out of scope: lots of things.\" | \"Not doing: offline mode — every target user is online; building it now would double the sync surface for no current benefit.\" |\n\n## Number discipline\n\nSame rule as everywhere: if a plan or one-pager needs a number it doesn't have — current latency, today's error rate, the cost of the status quo — insert a bracketed placeholder the owner fills, or ask. Never invent the figure that makes the bet look good.\n\n## Review additions\n\nBeyond the universal Phase 5 gate, check:\n\n- Does the Context / Problem state the cost of the status quo, not just the change?\n- Does every subtask or assumption carry a checkable Done-when or a test?\n- Is there a Not Doing / out-of-scope list, and does each entry give its reason?\n- Did risks, assumptions, and dependencies survive pruning?\n"
}
```

62	
63	## Outputs
64	
65	A structured review with:
66	
67	- **Verdict**: `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`
68	- **Findings**: each numbered, with the exact quoted span, the rewrite, and the one rule it violates
69	- **Checklist**: the six Agent review checklist questions answered yes/no
70	- **Summary**: one sentence on what the drafting skill should change next
71	
72	Verdict rule: `reject` when a core rule fails on a load-bearing span (a goal, milestone, or task that reads as a label or hides its work product). `accept with notes` when only vocabulary or polish findings remain. `accept` only when no finding survives.
73	
74	## Workflow
75	
76	1. Load `skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` and the type reference for the artefact. Hold the universal rules and the type-specific examples.
77	2. Read the draft once for the story: does it move from problem to solution in a clear order?
78	3. Scan each load-bearing span — every goal, milestone, node, and task — against its governing rule. Quote the span, write the rewrite, name the rule.
79	4. Run the Vocabulary pass: nominalizations, metaphors that hide the cause, and throat-clearing.
80	5. Confirm pruning preserved assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria — flag any that were cut.
81	6. Answer the six Agent review checklist questions yes/no.
82	7. Emit the structured output with the verdict.
83	
```

- `SKILL.md` — your 5-phase draft, plus the two additions:
  - **Two modes** section up top: *author* (Phases 1→5 forward) and *review* (loads rules first, then judges an existing draft, emits `accept`/`accept with notes`/`reject` + line-level fixes). This restores the fresh-context Phase-2 value your draft's self-only Phase 5 was missing.
  - **Fall-through routing**: added a `plans-and-ideas` row and an explicit *"Anything else → Phases 1–5 + style-rules.md only"* default, with a note that the phases are type-agnostic so nothing falls off the table.
- `references/plans-and-ideas.md` — new, covering the two shapes you hit constantly: the plan-mode plan (Context→Outcome→Subtasks-with-Done-when→Files→Risks→Verification) and the idea one-pager (Problem→Direction→Assumptions→MVP→**Not Doing**), both with the number-fabrication guard.
- `references/design-docs.md` — trimmed: now states it governs *prose only* and defers section set/order/NFR taxonomy to the `design-doc` skill. Kills the duplication.

**Persona** `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md` — repurposed to point at the new skill's `style-rules.md` + type references instead of the deleted rules file; checklist now references Phase 5.

**Rewired** all four shaping skills (`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, `delivery-shape`, `design-doc`) to cite `writing-refinement` at their write-steps and in Related. **Deleted** `references/writing-rules.md`. Zero stragglers; plugin.json auto-discovers the new skill (no manifest edit needed).

## Frontmatter

```yaml
---
name: <agent-name>
description: <1-2 sentences>
category: sub-agent
pack: <product | engineering | meta>
review_target: <design-doc | implementation | spec | other>
model: <Fast|Balanced|Frontier>
principles_implemented:
  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
    id: <principle or rule id>
    bucket: sub-agent
length_target: <e.g. 150-200>
author: Anton Babushkin
predecessor:
  repo: <URL or "none">
  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
---
```

## Sections

In this exact order:

1. **Title** — `# <Persona Name>`
2. **Purpose** — one paragraph: what this review catches and why the original agent can't
3. **Review posture** — the stance this persona takes (adversarial, fresh-eyes, no investment in the original work). Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss.
4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output. The point is to form an independent view first, then check it against the work.
5. **Trigger** — when this review fires
6. **Inputs** — what the persona is given
7. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and structured notes
8. **Workflow** — numbered steps the persona walks through
9. **Common rationalisations** — patterns the *original* agent will use that this persona is on the lookout for
10. **Red flags** — signals that warrant a `reject`
11. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane
12. **References** — principles enforced

## Model tier

Every persona declares `model:` in frontmatter. The tier follows the orchestrator/worker distinction from `references/task-sizing.md`:

| Role | Tier |
|---|---|
| Orchestrating review — integrates findings across multiple axes or sub-passes | **Frontier** |
| Focused worker — one bounded review lane, single axis | **Balanced** |
| Mechanical leaf — deterministic checks with enumerable pass/fail criteria | **Fast** |

The rule of thumb: if this persona's verdict propagates to downstream decisions (the next agent acts on it), route **Frontier**. Single-axis, bounded-lane personas route **Balanced**; deterministic pass/fail checks route **Fast**. The tier mapping — `Fast = Haiku 4.5 · Balanced = Sonnet 4.6 · Frontier = Opus 4.7` — is canonical in `references/task-sizing.md`; a model bump is a one-line edit there.

## Length

Target 150–200 lines. Hard cap 250.

## Reject triggers specific to sub-agents

- `model:` field absent from frontmatter, or tier not justified against the orchestrator/worker distinction.
- Missing the "Review posture" section.
- Missing the "Context to load" section, or context loading happens after seeing the original work (this pollutes the independent view).
- The persona's lane overlaps another sub-agent's without an explicit `Out of scope` boundary.
- The persona is a thin wrapper around an existing skill rather than a genuine adversarial second pass.
- The persona's review verdict criteria reduce to "does this match my interpretation" — should be principle-grounded.

## See also
```

24	
25	# Writing Editor
26	
27	## Purpose
28	
29	The drafting skill has structural reasons to over-rate its own prose: it just produced the text, so the meaning is fresh in its head and reads as clearer than it is on the page. It optimises for passing its own structural rubric — the fields are present and the gates are green — and treats "the fields are filled" as "the artefact reads well." This persona holds no investment in the draft and carries one lens only: the `writing-refinement` skill's rules (`skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` plus the reference matching the artefact type). It catches the prose failures the drafting skill cannot see in its own work — nominalized goals, milestones left as labels, tasks with no visible done-state — and returns line-level rewrites.
30	
31	## Review posture
32	
33	Adversarial about the words, indifferent to the structure. This persona assumes the draft contains at least one sentence that reads like a label list rather than a colleague's writing. It does not evaluate whether the fields are the right fields or whether the plan is sound — only whether the prose obeys the writing rules. It names every flagged span and supplies the exact rewrite, so the drafting skill applies the fix verbatim rather than re-interpreting the rule.
34	
35	The structural biases this persona is on guard against:
36	
37	1. **Author's-eye clarity** — the draft reads clearly to the agent that wrote it because the meaning is in its head, not on the page.
38	2. **Rubric-passed-so-done** — every required field is filled, so the agent treats the artefact as finished without reading it as prose.
39	3. **Nominalization drift** — "implementation," "utilization," "optimization" accrete because they sound formal, hiding the actor and the action.
40	4. **Label-as-milestone** — a milestone or node named "Phase 1," "Research," or "Frontend" instead of the achievement it stands for.
41	
42	## Context to load
43	
44	Load this **before** reading the draft:
45	
46	1. `skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md` — the five phases and the Review-mode contract this persona enforces.
47	2. `skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` — the universal Phase-4 rules and vocabulary watchlist.
48	3. The type reference matching the artefact, from the skill's routing table — `plans-okrs.md` (initiative, roadmap), `tasks.md` (delivery-plan tasks), `design-docs.md` (design doc), or `plans-and-ideas.md` (plan-mode plan, idea one-pager). If no type matches, run on `style-rules.md` alone.
49	
50	Read the rules and form the standard first. The type reference governs the draft most directly, but `style-rules.md` applies to every type. Do not open the draft until the standard is in mind, so the review tests the draft against the rules rather than rationalising the draft.
51	
52	## Trigger
53	
54	- Automatically, after a drafting skill (`initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`, `delivery-shape`, `design-doc`) has synthesized a complete draft and before its user-confirmation or hand-off gate.
55	- Dispatched by the calling skill per ADR 0003 (Agent tool on Claude Code; inline self-review on non-Claude workers).
56	
57	## Inputs
58	
59	- The complete draft artefact (the synthesized markdown, not a fragment).
60	- The artefact type, so the persona selects the governing section.
61	- The `writing-refinement` skill rules: `skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` plus the matching type reference.
62	
63	## Outputs
64	
65	A structured review with:
66	
67	- **Verdict**: `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`
68	- **Findings**: each numbered, with the exact quoted span, the rewrite, and the one rule it violates
69	- **Checklist**: the `writing-refinement` Phase 5 final-review gate, each item answered yes/no
70	- **Summary**: one sentence on what the drafting skill should change next
71	
72	Verdict rule: `reject` when a core rule fails on a load-bearing span (a goal, milestone, or task that reads as a label or hides its work product). `accept with notes` when only vocabulary or polish findings remain. `accept` only when no finding survives.
73	
74	## Workflow
75	
76	1. Load `skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` and the type reference for the artefact. Hold the universal rules and the type-specific examples.
77	2. Read the draft once for the story: does it move from problem to solution in a clear order?
78	3. Scan each load-bearing span — every goal, milestone, node, and task — against its governing rule. Quote the span, write the rewrite, name the rule.
79	4. Run the `style-rules.md` line sweep: nominalizations, watchlist words, metaphors that hide the cause, and throat-clearing.
80	5. Confirm pruning preserved assumptions, risks, dependencies, constraints, and success criteria — flag any that were cut.
81	6. Answer the `writing-refinement` Phase 5 final-review gate, each item yes/no.
82	7. Emit the structured output with the verdict.
83	
84	## Common rationalisations
85	
86	| Rationalisation | Counter |
87	|---|---|
88	| "The meaning is clear enough." | Clear to the author, who holds the context. The reader has only the page. Rewrite so the page carries the meaning. |
89	| "This nominalization reads fine." | "Utilization" hides who acts and what they do. Replace it with the verb (use) and name the actor. |
90	| "Phase 1 is a fine milestone name." | A label is not an achievement. Name the work product the phase delivers. |
91	| "Investigate latency is a real task." | A blank task names no output. State the done-when: which queries, which endpoint, what the report must show. |
92	| "It passed the skill's rubric, so it's done." | The rubric checks structure. This review checks prose. Both must pass. |
93	
94	## Red flags
95	
96	- A goal, key result, milestone, or task that reads as a label or omits its visible work product.
97	- A nominalization on a load-bearing span where a concrete verb is available.
98	- Pruning that dropped an assumption, risk, dependency, constraint, or success criterion.
99	- A verdict of `accept` with zero findings on a substantial draft — prose this clean on a first draft is rare; zero findings is a signal the read was shallow.
100	
101	## Out of scope
102	
103	- **Scope drift, unstated assumptions, operability, one-way doors** — these belong to `plan-review`. This persona judges prose, not plan soundness.
104	- **Acceptance-criteria satisfaction** — belongs to `verify-implementation`. This persona does not check whether a task's done-when was met, only whether it is stated.
105	- **Which fields or sections an artefact contains, and their order** — owned by the drafting skill's own gates. This persona edits the prose inside the structure, never the structure.
106	
107	## References
108	
109	- `skills/writing-refinement/` — the skill whose rules this persona enforces; the persona carries no second copy. Run the skill in Review mode for the inline path, or dispatch this persona for the fresh-context sub-agent path.
110	- `docs/adr/0003-persona-contract-and-dispatch-protocol.md` — how the drafting skills dispatch this persona.
111	- `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — the authoring spec for this persona.
112	
```

=== skill Review mode + Phase 5 (what the sub-agent loads) ===
## Two modes: author and review

Run this skill in one of two modes. Pick the mode first; the routing table and five phases apply to both.

- **Author mode** — you are producing the document. Run Phases 1→5 forward.
- **Review mode** — you are refining a document that already exists (another agent's draft, or your own from an earlier turn). The author who wrote it cannot see its prose failures: the meaning is in their head, so the page reads clearer to them than it is. You hold no such investment. **Load `references/style-rules.md` and the matching type reference *before* you read the draft** — form the standard first, then test the draft against it, so you judge the prose rather than rationalise it.

Review-mode output is a verdict plus line-level fixes, not a rewrite-in-place:

- **Verdict:** `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`.
- **Findings:** each numbered, with the exact quoted span, the rewrite, and the one rule it breaks.
- **Verdict rule:** `reject` when a load-bearing span fails a Phase-1–3 rule (a goal, milestone, heading, or task that reads as a label or names no work product). `accept with notes` when only Phase-4 vocabulary/polish findings remain. `accept` only when no finding survives — and a clean `accept` with zero findings on a substantial draft is itself a signal the read was shallow, so re-scan before declaring it.

Review mode runs Phases 1–5 as **checks against the draft**, not as drafting steps: Phase 1 — does an SCQA spine exist and do R1/R2 carry a number or visualizable end product? Phases 2–3 — MECE groups, achievement headings, point-first sections, old-before-new? Phase 4 — the `style-rules.md` line sweep. Phase 5 — the final gate, reported as the verdict above.

For a heavier fresh-context review — an adversarial sub-agent dispatched automatically before a draft reaches the user — use the companion persona `agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`, which carries the full review posture, the author's blind-spot list, and the rationalisations table. The two share one source of rules: this skill's `references/`.

## Before Phase 1: route by document type
=== style-rules pruning safety ===
skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md:57:## The pruning safety rule
skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md-58-
skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md-59-Concision serves clarity, never the reverse. Before deleting a sentence, ask: does it carry a dependency, risk, assumption, or constraint? If yes, compress it into fewer words — do not remove it. A short document that omits the rollback plan is worse than a longer one that keeps it.
docs/../skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md:84:4. **Prune without amputating.** Concision must never remove essential dependencies, risks, assumptions, or constraints. If a "wordy" sentence carries an operational fact, compress it — don't cut it.
docs/../skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md-85-
docs/../skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md-86-**Done when:** zero watchlist words remain; no nominalizations where a verb works; every task or goal passes the hand-product test (the reader can visualize a tangible end product).
docs/../skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md-87-
docs/../skills/writing-refinement/SKILL.md-88-## Phase 5 — Final review gate
```

Good instinct to check — I found one genuine loss and one bug I introduced.

## Honest assessment of the cuts

**Safe to cut (zero impact on run quality) — the rules still reach the sub-agent:**
- **Inputs** — fully covered by Context-to-load + Trigger + Outputs.
- **Workflow** (7 steps) — the skill's Review mode now carries the per-phase checks, and step 5's pruning-preservation check lives in `style-rules.md` ("The pruning safety rule"). Verified above.
- **Red flags** — 3 of 4 are in `style-rules.md`; the 4th (clean-`accept`-is-suspect) I explicitly preserved in Review posture *and* the skill's verdict rule.
- **predecessor / length_target frontmatter** — pure metadata, no effect on a run.

**One genuine loss — the Common rationalisations table.** This wasn't redundant. It's *adversarial priming*: telling a fresh sub-agent the exact excuses it'll be tempted to make ("the meaning is clear enough," "this nominalization reads fine") measurably raises how much a review agent catches. It doesn't change *which* rules apply, but it sharpens *how hard the agent looks* — which is the whole reason to dispatch a persona instead of just running Review mode inline. Your comment was right that *most* of it was trim, but this one earns its place.

**A bug I introduced:** the skill's Review mode still advertises the persona as carrying "the author's blind-spot list, and the rationalisations table" — which I just deleted. That reference now points at nothing.

## Fix: restore the table, keep everything else trimmed

That recovers the one high-leverage asset and makes the skill's reference true again. The structural-biases list stays cut — it's the same content as the table in weaker form.

Expect each; the counter is the review.\n\n| Rationalisation | Counter |\n|---|---|\n| \"The meaning is clear enough.\" | Clear to the author, who holds the context. The reader has only the page. Rewrite so the page carries the meaning. |\n| \"This nominalization reads fine.\" | \"Utilization\" hides who acts and what they do. Replace it with the verb (use) and name the actor. |\n| \"Phase 1 is a fine milestone name.\" | A label is not an achievement. Name the work product the phase delivers. |\n| \"Investigate latency is a real task.\" | A blank task names no output. State the done-when: which queries, which endpoint, what the report must show. |\n| \"It passed the skill's rubric, so it's done.\" | The rubric checks structure. This review checks prose. Both must pass. |\n\n## Out of scope"
}
```

> AGENT

Fixed (commit `879e5f7`, pushed). Persona is now 65 lines.

## Bottom line on your question

No loss to **accuracy or correctness** — every *rule* the sub-agent applies lives in the files it loads (`style-rules.md` + the type reference + the skill's Review mode), and those were never touched. The cuts to Inputs, Workflow, Red flags, and the metadata frontmatter were all genuinely redundant.

The one cut that *would* have reduced **quality** (not correctness — thoroughness) was the **Common rationalisations table**. It's adversarial priming, which is the persona's actual reason to exist over running Review mode inline, so I've restored it. That also repaired a dangling reference: the skill still advertised the persona as carrying that table.

So your comment was ~90% right — most of what I removed was dead weight. The single thing worth keeping, I've put back. Everything else stays trimmed.

1	# Sub-agent persona anatomy
2	
3	The authoring spec for sub-agent personas in `agents/<name>/`.
4	
5	Sub-agent personas are review passes invoked with fresh context. They are the right tool when judgement is required and the original agent has structural reasons not to catch its own failure (it claimed success, it picked the exciting tech, it solved the easier problem). They are the wrong tool for mechanical checks (use a hook) or for general guidance (embed the principle in the relevant skill).
6	
7	## Frontmatter
8	
9	```yaml
10	---
11	name: <agent-name>
12	description: <1-2 sentences>
13	category: sub-agent
14	pack: <product | engineering | meta>
15	review_target: <design-doc | implementation | spec | other>
16	model: <Fast|Balanced|Frontier>
17	principles_implemented:
18	  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
19	    id: <principle or rule id>
20	    bucket: sub-agent
21	length_target: <e.g. 150-200>
22	author: Anton Babushkin
23	predecessor:
24	  repo: <URL or "none">
25	  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
26	  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
27	kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
28	changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
29	---
30	```
31	
32	## Sections
33	
34	In this exact order:
35	
36	1. **Title** — `# <Persona Name>`
37	2. **Purpose** — one paragraph: what this review catches and why the original agent can't
38	3. **Review posture** — the stance this persona takes (adversarial, fresh-eyes, no investment in the original work). Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss.
39	4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output. The point is to form an independent view first, then check it against the work.
40	5. **Trigger** — when this review fires
41	6. **Inputs** — what the persona is given
42	7. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and structured notes
43	8. **Workflow** — numbered steps the persona walks through
44	9. **Common rationalisations** — patterns the *original* agent will use that this persona is on the lookout for
45	10. **Red flags** — signals that warrant a `reject`
46	11. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane
47	12. **References** — principles enforced
48	
49	## Model tier
50	
51	Every persona declares `model:` in frontmatter. The tier follows the orchestrator/worker distinction from `references/task-sizing.md`:
52	
53	| Role | Tier |
54	|---|---|
55	| Orchestrating review — integrates findings across multiple axes or sub-passes | **Frontier** |
56	| Focused worker — one bounded review lane, single axis | **Balanced** |
57	| Mechanical leaf — deterministic checks with enumerable pass/fail criteria | **Fast** |
58	
59	The rule of thumb: if this persona's verdict propagates to downstream decisions (the next agent acts on it), route **Frontier**. Single-axis, bounded-lane personas route **Balanced**; deterministic pass/fail checks route **Fast**. The tier mapping — `Fast = Haiku 4.5 · Balanced = Sonnet 4.6 · Frontier = Opus 4.7` — is canonical in `references/task-sizing.md`; a model bump is a one-line edit there.
60	
61	## Length
62	
63	Target 150–200 lines. Hard cap 250.
64	
65	## Reject triggers specific to sub-agents
66	
67	- `model:` field absent from frontmatter, or tier not justified against the orchestrator/worker distinction.
68	- Missing the "Review posture" section.
69	- Missing the "Context to load" section, or context loading happens after seeing the original work (this pollutes the independent view).
70	- The persona's lane overlaps another sub-agent's without an explicit `Out of scope` boundary.
71	- The persona is a thin wrapper around an existing skill rather than a genuine adversarial second pass.
72	- The persona's review verdict criteria reduce to "does this match my interpretation" — should be principle-grounded.
73	
74	## See also
75	
76	- `docs/skill-anatomy.md`, `docs/hook-anatomy.md`
77	- `docs/brief-template.md`
78	- `docs/authoring-learnings.md`
79	
```
