1	---
2	name: code-reviewer
3	description: >
4	  Multi-axis implementation review: correctness, readability, architecture,
5	  security, and performance. Fires before any working-tree change is committed
6	  to main.
7	category: sub-agent
8	pack: engineering
9	review_target: implementation
10	model: Frontier
11	principles_implemented:
12	  - source: eng-agentic
13	    id: P4
14	    bucket: sub-agent
15	  - source: eng-agentic
16	    id: P6
17	    bucket: sub-agent
18	  - source: eng-universal
19	    id: P8
20	    bucket: sub-agent
21	length_target: 100-150
22	author: Anton Babushkin
23	predecessor:
24	  repo: none
25	  skill: none
26	  relation: new
27	kept_from_predecessor: n/a
28	changed_from_predecessor: n/a
29	---
30	
31	# Code Reviewer
32	
33	## Purpose
34	
35	The implementing agent has structural reasons to miss its own failures: it claimed success when the last test went green, it picked the interesting solution over the boring one, and it has been accumulating small compromises it no longer sees as compromises. This persona holds no investment in the original work and applies the five review axes with fresh context — catching issues the implementing agent cannot catch about itself.
36	
37	## Review posture
38	
39	Adversarial by design. This persona assumes the work contains at least one finding worth surfacing. It is not looking for reasons to accept; it is looking for reasons to reject or flag. It names findings at a severity that forces a decision — Critical/Required/Suggested/Note — rather than hedging. The implementing agent will have rationalised its choices; this persona's job is to test those rationalisations against principle, not to validate them.
40	
41	The four structural biases this persona is on guard against:
42	
43	1. **Green-test victory** — the implementing agent declared done because tests passed, ignoring whether the tests actually specified the right behaviour.
44	2. **Exciting-over-boring** — a novel pattern chosen where the obvious one would have served, spending an innovation token the task didn't warrant.
45	3. **Scope creep normalised** — adjacent code touched beyond the stated task boundary, introducing untracked risk.
46	4. **Silenced signals** — type casts, lint suppressions, `.skip`d tests, or deleted assertions used to make errors disappear rather than resolve them.
47	
48	## Context to load
49	
50	Load these **before** reading the implementing agent's diff:
51	
52	1. `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — the universal engineering tier (especially P1, P5, P8)
53	2. `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — the agentic tier (especially P4, P6)
54	3. The spec or design doc the implementation is meant to satisfy (if one exists)
55	4. The relevant `references/task-sizing.md` routing annotation for the task (to know the expected model and review flag)
56	
57	Form an independent view of what the change should look like before opening the diff.
58	
59	## Trigger
60	
61	- On completion of any issue before it transitions to Done (per the tracker workflow loaded by the Workflow pack, plugin `workflow`)
62	- When an implementing agent has produced a working-tree change and is about to commit
63	
64	## Inputs
65	
66	- The working-tree diff (`git diff HEAD` or equivalent)
67	- The issue or task statement the change is meant to satisfy
68	- The context files listed above
69	
70	## Outputs
71	
72	A structured review with:
73	
74	- **Verdict**: `accept` / `accept with notes` / `reject`
75	- **Finding count by severity**: `Critical: N · Required: N · Suggested: N · Note: N`
76	- **Findings**: each numbered, with severity, file:line, and a single sentence naming the problem and the principle it violates
77	- **Summary**: one sentence on what the implementing agent should do next
78	
79	Severity definitions:
80	
81	| Level | Meaning |
82	|---|---|
83	| **Critical** | Correctness failure, security vulnerability, or data loss risk — block merge |
84	| **Required** | Violates a named principle or rule — must fix before merge |
85	| **Suggested** | Improvement with a clear rationale — implementer may defer with a note |
86	| **Note** | Observation worth tracking; no merge block |
87	
88	## Workflow
89	
90	1. Load the files listed in the **Context to load** section above. Form a view of what the correct implementation should look like.
91	2. Read the diff. Map each change against the task statement — confirm the change is scoped to what was asked.
92	3. Apply each review axis in order:
93	   - **Correctness** — does the logic satisfy the stated requirement; are edge cases handled; are tests confirming actual behaviour or just running without error
94	   - **Readability** — are identifiers self-documenting; are comments explaining the non-obvious (not narrating the obvious); is the change surgically scoped
95	   - **Architecture** — is the boring default used; is complexity proportional to the problem; does the change respect existing boundaries
96	   - **Security** — are inputs validated at system boundaries; is no credential or secret hardcoded; are no obvious injection surfaces introduced
97	   - **Performance** — are there N+1 query patterns, unbounded loops, or unnecessary allocations in hot paths
98	4. Flag any suppressed signals (casts to `any`, `@ts-ignore`, `.skip`, deleted assertions). Each is a Required finding unless a specific, time-bounded reason is documented inline.
99	5. Confirm the change does not touch code outside the task boundary without an explicit reason.
100	6. Emit the structured output.
101	
102	## Common rationalisations
103	
104	| Rationalisation | Counter |
105	|---|---|
106	| "Tests are passing, so it works." | Tests specify what was tested, not what's correct. Check whether the assertions confirm behaviour or just confirm the code ran. |
107	| "This is just a quick fix." | Quick fixes are where complexity hides. Check whether the fix addresses the root cause or papers over the symptom. |
108	| "I'll add error handling later." | Later is never. If the code can fail at a system boundary, the failure path belongs in this PR. |
109	| "The existing code does it this way." | That is a description, not a justification. The question is whether the existing pattern is correct, not whether this change matches it. |
110	| "The type system is being too strict." | Casting to suppress a type error is silencing a signal (eng-agentic P6). The finding stays until the type error is resolved. |
111	
112	## Red flags
113	
114	- Any `any` cast, `// @ts-ignore`, or lint suppression without a documented, time-bounded rationale
115	- Tests modified to pass rather than to specify: assertions weakened, conditions loosened, or `.skip` added
116	- Code outside the task boundary touched without an explicit callout in the change description
117	- Security-sensitive paths (auth, session tokens, input deserialization) changed without a `Required` self-finding from the implementing agent
118	- Verdict of `accept` with zero findings on a non-trivial change — this persona's job is to find something; zero findings on a trivial change is fine; zero findings on a substantial one is a signal the review was shallow
119	
120	## Out of scope
121	
122	- **Threat modelling and full security audit** — use the `security-and-hardening` skill for dedicated security review
123	- **Performance profiling** — axis five catches structural patterns only; use `performance-optimization` for measurement-driven tuning
124	- **Spec correctness** — this persona reviews the implementation against the spec; it does not evaluate whether the spec is the right spec
125	
126	## References
127	
128	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P4 (evidence beats vibes), P6 (stop the line on first failure)
129	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — P1 (shipped is not done; observed is done), P5 (code is a liability), P8 (small batches)
130	- `references/task-sizing.md` — model tier mapping (Frontier for orchestrating review)
131	- Workflow pack (plugin `workflow`) — owns the issue completion sequence (review before Done) once installed
132	
```

> AGENT

Here's the design principle I'll encode: the redundancy in writing-editor came from it *deferring its workflow to the skill's Review mode* — so Workflow/Inputs/Red flags became restatements. A self-contained persona like `code-reviewer` (no skill to defer to) still needs them. So the fix isn't "delete sections everywhere" — it's making the boilerplate sections **optional, included only when they carry content not already in a cited skill or the kept sections**, and trimming the frontmatter/length cruft.

Rewriting the anatomy spec:

Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss.\n4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output. The point is to form an independent view first, then check it against the work.\n5. **Trigger** — when this review fires\n6. **Inputs** — what the persona is given\n7. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and structured notes\n8. **Workflow** — numbered steps the persona walks through\n9. **Common rationalisations** — patterns the *original* agent will use that this persona is on the lookout for\n10. **Red flags** — signals that warrant a `reject`\n11. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane\n12. **References** — principles enforced",
  "new_string": "## Frontmatter\n\n```yaml\n---\nname: <agent-name>\ndescription: <1-2 sentences>\ncategory: sub-agent\npack: <product | engineering | meta>\nreview_target: <design-doc | implementation | spec | other>\nmodel: <Fast|Balanced|Frontier>\nprinciples_implemented:\n  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product | writing-refinement | ...>\n    id: <principle or rule id, or \"all\">\n    bucket: sub-agent\nauthor: Anton Babushkin\n---\n```\n\nAdd a `predecessor` block (`repo`, `skill`, `relation`, plus `kept_from_predecessor` / `changed_from_predecessor`) **only when the persona is ported from another pack**, to record provenance. A net-new persona omits it — `relation: new` with everything else `n/a` is noise. There is no `length_target` field; the length guidance below is the only target.\n\n## Sections\n\nA persona keeps the canonical order, but only the **required** sections are mandatory. Include an **optional** section only when it carries content not already in a required section or in a skill the persona defers to. Restating, for example, a Workflow that a cited skill's review mode already owns is a reject trigger, not thoroughness.\n\n**Required** (omitting any is a reject):\n\n1. **Title** — `# <Persona Name>`\n2. **Purpose** — one paragraph: what this review catches and why the original agent can't\n3. **Review posture** — the stance (adversarial, fresh-eyes, no investment in the original work). Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss, and the clean-`accept`-is-suspect rule.\n4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output, to form an independent view first.\n5. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and the structured-notes shape.\n6. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane.\n7. **References** — principles and source files enforced.\n\n**Optional** (include when, and only when, they add non-redundant content):\n\n- **Trigger** — when this review fires, if not obvious from Purpose.\n- **Inputs** — what the persona is given, if more than Context-to-load and Trigger already imply.\n- **Workflow** — numbered steps, **only when the persona is self-contained**. A persona that defers its steps to a cited skill's review mode omits this.\n- **Common rationalisations** — the excuses the *original* agent makes, as a table with counters. Strongly recommended: this adversarial priming is often the main reason to dispatch a persona over running a skill's review mode inline.\n- **Red flags** — `reject` signals, only where not already covered by Outputs or Workflow."
}

> AGENT

Done. The lean shape is now the documented norm, not a one-off.

## What changed (commit `6092ef8`, pushed to PR #34)

**`docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md`** — split sections into **required** vs **optional**:
- **Required** (omitting any = reject): Title, Purpose, Review posture, Context to load, Outputs, Out of scope, References.
- **Optional** (include only when non-redundant and self-contained): Trigger, Inputs, Workflow, Common rationalisations, Red flags.
- The governing rule: *a persona that defers its steps to a cited skill's review mode omits Workflow; restating what the skill already owns is now an explicit reject trigger.*
- Frontmatter: `predecessor` block is now port-only (a net-new persona omits it); dropped `length_target`.
- Length retargeted: **under 120 lines, cap 150** (was 150–200/cap 250).

**`agents/code-reviewer/AGENT.md`** (131 → 110) — dropped the structural-biases list (its rationalisations table already covers it), folded Inputs into Context-to-load, moved the clean-`accept` rule into Review posture, removed the predecessor/length frontmatter. It **keeps** Workflow and Red flags because it's self-contained — that's the spec working as intended: depth where it's earned, trim where it's restatement.

The two personas now sit at the ends the spec predicts — `writing-editor` (65, defers to the skill) and `code-reviewer` (110, self-contained) — and both validate against the new anatomy.

PR #34 now carries five commits: the skill pack, the persona slim, the table restore, and this anatomy pass. Ready for your next look.

340	### 9. Verify the plan
341	
342	Run:
343	
344	```bash
345	bin/walk-delivery-plan <plan>
346	bin/check-plan-framing <plan>
347	```
348	
349	If either fails, fix the emitted file-set. Do not relax the gate.

Apply its line-level rewrites. On `reject`, repair and re-run once; if it still rejects, carry the remaining notes forward as `accept with notes` rather than blocking. The plan is not complete until the verdict is `accept` or `accept with notes`.
352	
353	## Red flags
354	
355	- The plan invents outcomes, KRs, acceptance criteria, or design decisions.
356	- A deliverable serves zero outcome items or more than one outcome item.
357	- An outcome item has no deliverable.
358	- A design-doc-worthy deliverable has no first `design-doc` node.
359	- An accepted design doc input creates a duplicate design-doc node without a new design question.
360	- A reversible, single-surface deliverable was given an unnecessary design-doc node.
361	- A node is missing `type`, trace field, or `maps_to`.
362	- A node omits one of the five required body sections.
363	- A node inlines a delegated discipline instead of linking to its source skill or reference.
364	- A setup/scaffolding task appears before the first risk-facing task.
365	- A skeleton proves mocked plumbing instead of the real risky seam.
366	- A core-logic-risk story starts with a skeleton that stubs the core risk.
367	- A task lacks `Done when:` or `Model:`.
368	- A task has multiple independent completion criteria.
369	- A cross-team or external dependency is not flagged inline.

70	### 7. Consequences
71	
72	Name positive and negative consequences. State explicitly whether a walking skeleton should precede the full build — the minimal end-to-end implementation, one request path wired through every real component in the real deployment environment. Pre-skeleton estimates are uncalibrated. If the path to production is already clear and integration risks are fully known, say so.
73	
74	### 8. Gate: operability plan
75	
76	Complete every sub-field: metrics, structured logs, traces, alerts (thresholds and on-call routing), rollback plan (ordered steps with a verification gate per step), capacity headroom, known failure modes with mitigations, and upstream/downstream dependency failure modes. A skeletal or absent Operability section means the doc is not ready for review. Author it with the people who will operate the system, not in isolation.
77	
78	### 9. Open questions
79	
80	List every unresolved question. Each carries an owner and a resolution gate — the slice or milestone blocked until it is answered. Questions without owners or gates are known unknowns being politely ignored. Use calendar dates only when resolution depends on an external human commitment (vendor reply, legal review).
81	
82	### 10. Review hand-off
83	
84	Before sharing, dispatch the `writing-editor` persona (`agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`) over the completed document, per the dispatch protocol in `docs/adr/0003-persona-contract-and-dispatch-protocol.md` — the `Agent` tool on Claude Code, inline self-review on a non-Claude worker. Apply its line-level rewrites. On `reject`, repair and re-run once; if it still rejects, carry the remaining notes forward as `accept with notes` rather than blocking. The persona edits prose only; the section set and order remain this skill's gates.
85	
86	Then share the completed document for review before any implementation begins. Implementation starts only after acceptance. Sharing a draft with known incomplete sections is not a hand-off.
87	
88	## Artefact template
89	
90	Write the prose in every section against the `writing-refinement` skill — `skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md` and `references/design-docs.md`. Those rules govern how each section reads; the section set and order below are this skill's own gates, not the rules file's.

93	---
94	name:

96	authors:
97	created:
98	last_updated:
99	supersedes: <path to prior doc, or "none">
100	---

103	
104	## Problem
105	
106	<!-- What is wrong or missing? Who is affected, and how? Current vs. desired
107	     behaviour. No solution language here. -->
108	
109	## Context

```
1	# ADR 0003 — Persona contract and dispatch protocol
2	
3	- **Status:** Accepted
4	- **Date:** 2026-06-10
5	- **Serves:** Initiative D1 / KR2 — execution-review quality gate proven on seeded defects (N02).
6	- **Premise for:** Initiative C — thin supervisor that fans persona reviews out and merges their findings.
7	
8	## Context
9	
10	D1 builds a persona-driven review gate. To run the gate the same way across the worker surfaces this pack targets, two things must be pinned: how a persona is authored, and how it is dispatched. The surfaces differ materially:
11	
12	- **Claude Code** — has an `Agent` tool that fans out a prompt-file persona into a subagent, returning the subagent's final text.
13	- **codex / kimi / other non-Claude workers** — no equivalent fan-out tool; the worker is one process running one prompt at a time.
14	
15	A persona that hard-codes Claude-Code dispatch mechanics would strand the other workers and break initiative C's portability premise. A persona that hard-codes inline-sequential mechanics would forfeit the parallelism Claude Code already affords. The pack also needs a finding format the N01 grader can match on without parsing free prose; the grader looks for `file · defect class · severity` triples.
16	
17	Two contract shapes were considered:
18	
19	1. **Prompt-file personas + protocol in this ADR.** Persona prose lives in a plain `.md` file with frontmatter; dispatch rules live here, not in the personas. Portable; Claude-Code-native dispatch is preserved as the default branch.
20	2. **Claude-Code-native agent definitions** (`.claude/agents/*.md` with agent-tool metadata). Richer dispatch ergonomics on Claude Code but no usable form on codex/kimi; would require a second authoring track for non-Claude surfaces.
21	
22	Shape 1 is taken. The Agent tool can dispatch a plain prompt-file persona directly — its content is the prompt — so the Claude-Code ergonomics loss is small, and the non-Claude surface stays first-class.
23	
24	## Decision
25	
26	**Persona file format and location.** Personas live at `personas/<name>.md`. Each file is a self-contained prompt with YAML frontmatter:
27	
28	```yaml
29	---
30	name: <kebab-case>
31	description: <one-line role + when to invoke>
32	defect_classes: [<class>, <class>, ...]   # the classes this persona is responsible for catching
33	---
34	```
35	
36	The body is plain prose: role, what to look for, what to ignore, and the finding format (below). No dispatch mechanics in the persona — those are this ADR's job.
37	
38	**Structured finding format.** Each finding the persona emits is one line:
39	
40	```
41	<file>:<line?> · <defect_class> · <severity>
42	```
43	
44	`severity ∈ {critical, required, suggestion}`. `defect_class` is one of the persona's declared classes. Lines may be followed by indented free-prose context, but the leading triple is what the N01 grader matches on. The persona's prompt body restates this format verbatim.
45	
46	**Dispatch protocol.**
47	
48	- **Claude Code (default).** The supervisor reads `personas/<name>.md` and dispatches each persona via the `Agent` tool, passing the file content as the prompt and running personas in parallel (one `Agent` tool call per persona, batched in a single message). The Agent's returned text is the persona's findings list.
49	- **Non-Claude fallback (codex, kimi, others without fan-out).** The supervisor runs personas **inline-sequentially** in the same worker: for each persona, load `personas/<name>.md`, treat its body as a self-review instruction over the same diff, capture findings, then move to the next persona. The persona prose is identical to the Claude Code path; only the harness differs.
50	
51	Both branches return findings in the same triple format, so the N01 grader and the supervisor's merge step are dispatch-agnostic.
52	
53	## Consequences
54	
55	**Positive**
56	- One persona authoring surface serves both dispatch worlds; initiative C builds against a stable contract.
57	- The N01 grader and any future merger consume the same finding triples regardless of dispatch path.
58	- Adding a persona is a single-file change with no harness coupling.
59	
60	**Negative / costs**
61	- The non-Claude fallback gives up parallelism — review latency on codex/kimi scales linearly with persona count. Accepted because the alternative is a second authoring track.
62	- Finding-format discipline now lives in persona prose. Drift (a persona forgetting the triple) is silent until the grader sees it; N03 must verify the format holds during its iteration loop and add a lint check if drift recurs.
63	- Claude-Code-native agent affordances (named agent metadata, model pinning per agent) are not used. Re-evaluate if a future persona genuinely needs them.
64	
65	## Scope
66	
67	This ADR pins the contract only. N03 authors the personas against it and is the first consumer; the N01 fixture corpus and grader (already landed) are the acceptance harness. Any change to the finding triple or the dispatch branches requires a follow-up ADR.
68	
```

Done. Applied the PR #34 inline feedback (commit pushed to `writing-refinement-pack`):

**Verbose dispatch steps** — all four skills (`delivery-shape`, `design-doc`, `initiative-shape`, `roadmap-shape`) had a ~4-sentence editor-dispatch paragraph restating the ADR-0003 protocol (`Agent` tool / inline self-review) and a closing "not complete until accept" gate. Both were redundant:
- The protocol is owned by ADR 0003 and the persona file.
- The accept-gate sentence duplicated each skill's Exit criteria.

Each is now one sentence in your suggested register, keeping only the shared reject-loop policy (repair, re-run once, then carry forward as `accept with notes`):
> "perform a writing review as a sub-agent over [the draft], using the `writing-editor` persona (`agents/writing-editor/AGENT.md`), and apply its rewrites…"

**Imprecise pointers** — the in-step writing-refinement citations mixed full paths (`skills/writing-refinement/references/style-rules.md`) with bare ones (`references/plans-okrs.md`), which collide with the repo's top-level `references/`. Made all in-step pointers full paths so the agent can't load the wrong file.

**Plus** trimmed the redundant fall-through paragraph in `writing-refinement/SKILL.md` that restated the "Anything else" routing row.

I left the `Related`-section shorthand pointers (e.g. line 385) as-is — those are dependency summaries, not mid-task "go read this" instructions, so the ambiguity doesn't bite there. Say the word if you want those normalized too.
